\frac{{\medcirc}\,a\hskip 2.8681pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.8681pta\hskip 8.19447ptF\,a\hskip 2.8681pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.8681pta}{{\medcirc}\,F^{*}a\hskip 2.8681pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.8681ptF^{*}a}\hskip 10.00002pt\textsc{Right={\hskip 5.0pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}orbit-desc}}}

\frac{a\hskip 2.8681pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.8681ptb\hskip 8.19447ptF\,b\hskip 2.8681pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.8681ptb}{F^{*}a\hskip 2.8681pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.8681ptb}\hskip 10.00002pt\textsc{right={\hskip 5.0pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$F^{*}$-introL}}}

\frac{a\hskip 2.8681pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.8681ptF\,a\hskip 8.19447pta\hskip 2.8681pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.8681ptb}{a\hskip 2.8681pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.8681ptF^{*}b}\hskip 10.00002pt\textsc{Right={\hskip 5.0pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$F^{*}$-introR}}}

Figure 3:
Additional axioms for the automation-friendly (and also more human-friendly) axiom system
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{1}
.
The axiom system
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{1}
contains
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{0}
(see
\Cref
fig:axioms-aic0) plus the additional axioms listed above.
All additional axioms listed here are
finitely
derivable from
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{0}
.

The extended axiom system
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{1}
is given by
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{0}
plus all axioms shown in
\Cref
fig:axioms-aic1.
Let us go over some of
these additional axioms
.

Additional Partial Order and Lattice Axioms

{\mathsf{AIC}}_{0}
axiomatizes algebraically the underlying bounded lattice.

{\mathsf{AIC}}_{1}
, through
reflex
,
trans
, and
antisymm
axiomatizes directly the induced partial order
\preceq
on the lattice.
We will sometimes write

\displaystyle\frac{a_{1}\hskip 2.8681pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.8681pta_{2}\hskip 8.19447pta_{2}\hskip 2.8681pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.8681pta_{3}\hskip 8.19447pt\ldots\hskip 8.19447pta_{k-1}\hskip 2.8681pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.8681pta_{k}}{a_{1}\hskip 2.8681pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.8681pta_{k}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{right={\hskip 3.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}trans${}^{*}$}}}\hskip 16.38895pt\textnormal{{\normalsize\raisebox{5.50003pt}{\large instead of}}}\hskip 16.38895pt\frac{a_{1}\hskip 2.8681pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.8681pta_{2}\hskip 8.19447pt\frac{a_{2}\hskip 1.71503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.71503pta_{3}\hskip 4.90001pt\frac{\vdots}{a_{3}\hskip 1.22502pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.22502pta_{k}}\hskip 4.90001pt\textsc{Right={\hskip 2.45pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}trans}}}}{a_{2}\hskip 1.71503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.71503pta_{k}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Right={\hskip 3.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}trans}}}}{a_{1}\hskip 2.8681pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.8681pta_{k}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Right={\hskip 3.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}trans}}}

as a shorthand for several intermediate applications of
trans
.

\frac{s\left[{a}\middle\backslash{u}\right]\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752ptt\left[{a}\middle\backslash{u}\right]\hskip 8.04996ptu\hskip 2.81752pt{}={}\hskip 2.81752ptw}{s\left[{a}\middle\backslash{w}\right]\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752ptt\left[{a}\middle\backslash{w}\right]}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}indiscern}}}

The
indiscern
axiom is morally the congruence rule of equational logic, but for inequalities and for arbitrary terms (not just flat ones of the form
\boxast\,(a,\,b,\ldots)
).
Intuitively, if
u=w
, then we can freely replace any occurrence of term
u
with term
w
in any inequality.
Formulating this as a rule is slightly more involved:
Let
s\preceq t
be the context in which the subterm
u
potentially occurs.
All to-be-replaced occurrences of
u
are represented by a placeholder variable
a
occurring instead of
u
in
s
and
t
and hence
s\left[{a}\middle\backslash{u}\right]\preceq t\left[{a}\middle\backslash{u}\right]
is the inequality in which occurrences of
u
are to be replaced with
w
.
If now
u=w
, we can actually make the replacement and conclude that if
s\left[{a}\middle\backslash{u}\right]\preceq t\left[{a}\middle\backslash{u}\right]
holds, then
also
s\left[{a}\middle\backslash{w}\right]\preceq t\left[{a}\middle\backslash{w}\right]
holds
.

\frac{a\curlyvee b\hskip 2.8681pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.8681ptb\curlywedge a\hskip 8.19447pta\hskip 2.8681pt{}={}\hskip 2.8681ptF\,a}{a\curlyvee b\hskip 2.8681pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.8681ptb\curlywedge\hbox{\pagecolor{DodgerBlue3!30!white}$F\,a$}}\hskip 10.00002pt\textsc{Left={{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}indiscern}\hskip 5.0pt}}

As an example, the derivation on the left is valid.
Here, term
s
is
a\curlyvee b
, term
t
is
b\curlywedge c
, and the placeholder is
c
.
The substitution instance
b\curlywedge F\,a
is obtained by replacing every
c
in
b\curlywedge c
by
F\,a
and
b\curlywedge a
is obtained by replacing
c
with
a
.
Notice that when reading the inference rule from top to bottom,
not
every occurrence
of
a
was replaced by
F\,a
.
This is because
s
was
a\curlyvee b
but placeholder
c
does not occur in
a\curlyvee b
.

Just for readability (but with
no
technical necessity), we write
b\curlywedge\hbox{\pagecolor{DodgerBlue3!30!white}$F\,a$}
(i.e. with a blue marking) below the inference bar to indicate where
F\,a
replaced
a
.
Intuitively,
indiscern
gives us the flexibility to freely select only
certain
occurrences of
u
for replacement by
w
and we indicate the locations were a replacement happened in
blue
.

\frac{c\left[{x}\middle\backslash{b\curlyvee a}\right]\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752ptd\left[{x}\middle\backslash{b\curlyvee a}\right]}{c\left[{x}\middle\backslash{a\curlyvee b}\right]\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752ptd\left[{x}\middle\backslash{a\curlyvee b}\right]}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$\curlyvee$-comm}}}

We will often use
indiscern
implicitly (but rigorously) in the
context of identity axioms:
Whenever we introduce an identity
abc
, we will implicitly introduce an inference rule which is derivable using
abc
as the right-hand premise of
indiscern
,
and
we will call this new inference rule also
abc
.
For example, the identity
\curlyvee
-comm
induces the rule
\curlyvee
-comm
shown in the box above.
Its derivation is shown below left.

asm.
indicates an assumption (i.e. a premise above the bar) of an inference rule / quasiequation that we want to derive.
Below right, we see an example of applying the implicit
\curlyvee
-comm
rule on
the left-hand-side (were we again indicate in
blue
the part to which the explicit
\curlyvee
-comm
identity was applied).

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{\textnormal{\footnotesize{\raisebox{-1.72221pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}{\color[rgb]{0.435,0.655,0.875}{asm.}}}}}}}{c\left[{x}\middle\backslash{b\curlyvee a}\right]\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752ptd\left[{x}\middle\backslash{b\curlyvee a}\right]}\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{\textnormal{\footnotesize{\raisebox{-1.72221pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$\curlyvee$-comm}}}}}{a\curlyvee b\hskip 2.81752pt{}={}\hskip 2.81752ptb\curlyvee a}}{c\left[{x}\middle\backslash{a\curlyvee b}\right]\hskip 3.23753pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.23753ptd\left[{x}\middle\backslash{a\curlyvee b}\right]}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Left={{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}indiscern}\hskip 4.5pt}}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\frac{b\curlyvee a\hskip 3.23753pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.23753ptb\curlywedge a}{\hbox{\pagecolor{DodgerBlue3!30!white}$a\curlyvee b$}\hskip 3.23753pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.23753ptb\curlywedge a}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$\curlyvee$-comm}}}

Additional Majora and Minora Axioms

By
\Diamond
-desc
and
\square
-asc
, majora form descending chains and minora form ascending chains.
In fact, we had mentioned earlier that the majorum
{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a
is the
least decreasing majorant
of
a
.
So if some sequence
b
is descending (
{\medcirc}\,b\preceq b
) and
b
is a majorant of
a
(
a\preceq b
), then
b
is also a majorant of
{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a
.
Dually the minorum
{{\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\square$}}}\,a
is
a
’s
greatest increasing minorant
.
The axioms
\Diamond
-introL
and
\square
-introR
capture precisely this.
Finally, much like in LTL, both
\Diamond
and
\square
each satisfy an
expansion law
.

Remark 12

(Majora-Minora Alternation Depth)
.

Just like in LTL
[
3
, Figure 5.7, absorption laws]
, multiple consecutive
\Diamond
’s or
\square
’s can be contracted to a single one (via idempotence), and the depth of alternating
\Diamond
’s and
\square
’s collapses at level 2, leaving only the two innermost operators relevant.
For instance,
{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,{{\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\square$}}}\,{{\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\square$}}}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a={{\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\square$}}}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a
. See
Appendix
D
for a more formal statement with proof.

\lipicsEnd

Additional Function Application and Orbit Axioms

\displaystyle\hskip-13.87491pt\frac{\frac{\frac{\textnormal{\footnotesize{\raisebox{-1.72221pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}${\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}$-inflate}}}}}{a\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503pt{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a}}{F\,a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752ptF\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$F$-mono}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{\frac{\frac{\textnormal{\footnotesize{\raisebox{-1.72221pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}${\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}$-desc}}}}}{{\medcirc}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503pt{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a}}{F\,{\medcirc}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptF\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a}\hskip 8.04996pt\textsc{Right={\hskip 3.15pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$F$-mono}}}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{DodgerBlue3!30!white}${\medcirc}\,F\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a$}\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752ptF\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$F{\medcirc}\,$-comm}}}}{{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F\,a\hskip 3.23753pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.23753ptF\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}${\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}$-introL}}}

Any monotonic function is
semi-continuous
.
In AIC, this reads
{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F\,a\preceq F\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a
, stated by
semi-cont
.
Dually,
semi-cocont
states

F\,{{\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\square$}}}\,a\preceq{{\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\square$}}}\,F\,a
.
To get a first feel for how to conduct actual AIC-style algebraic proofs, the proof tree above right shows the derivation of
semi-cont
from other axioms.

\frac{a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752pt{\medcirc}\,a}{F\,F^{*}a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752pt{\medcirc}\,F^{*}a}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}asc-iter}}}

\frac{{\medcirc}\,a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752pta}{{\medcirc}\,F^{*}a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752ptF\,F^{*}a}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}desc-iter}}}

As for additional orbit axioms, we can recover the
“naive”
version of
iter
, namely
{\medcirc}\,F^{*}a=F\,F^{*}a
, for flat sequences:
For ascending chains
a
, the two rule
asc-iter
yields
F\,F^{*}a\preceq{\medcirc}\,F^{*}a
, and for descending chains the rule
desc-iter
yields
{\medcirc}\,F^{*}a\preceq F\,F^{*}a
.
If
a
is now flat (
a\preceq{\medcirc}\,a\preceq a
), we obtain
{\medcirc}\,F^{*}a=F\,F^{*}a
.

The axioms
orbit-asc
and
orbit-desc
assert that certain orbits form chains.
This is of interest since chains always converge in our setting and we often want orbits to constitute fixed point iterations that converge to a fixed point.
If
a\preceq F\,a
and
a
is ascending, for instance, then the orbit
F^{*}a
is provably also ascending.
Dually, if
F\,a\preceq a
and
a
is descending,
F^{*}a
is also descending.

Finally, we can derive from
F
-ind
and
F
-coind
rules that are similar to
\Diamond
-introL
and
\square
-introR
, namely
F^{*}
-introR
and
F^{*}
-introL
.

Soundness of
\boldsymbol{{\mathsf{AIC}}_{1}}

We have the following theorem whose proof has been formalized in Isabelle/HOL:

Theorem 13

(Soundness of
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{1}
)
.

{\mathsf{AIC}}_{1}
is sound for sequence algebras.

Proof.

{\mathsf{AIC}}_{1}
contains all axioms of
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{0}
which are all valid for sequence algebras (see
\Cref
thm:soundness).
All additional axioms in
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{1}\setminus{\mathsf{AIC}}_{0}
are finitely derivable from
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{0}
and hence also valid for any model of
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{0}
.
For those derivations, see Appendix
C
.
∎

Continuous Functions

\frac{\textnormal{\footnotesize{\raisebox{-1.72221pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}c-cont}}}}}{F\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752pt{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F\,a}

\frac{\textnormal{\footnotesize{\raisebox{-1.72221pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}c-cocont}}}}}{{{\raisebox{-0.69296pt}{$\square$}}}\,F\,a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752ptF\,{{\raisebox{-0.69296pt}{$\square$}}}\,a}

From monotonicity, all functions are necessarily semi-continuous:
{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F\,a\preceq F\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a
.
If the other direction
F\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a\preceq{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F\,a
(and thus the identity
F\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a={\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F\,a
) holds, the function is
“fully”
continuous.
We can state that certain functions
F
are continuous by additionally postulating
non-derivable
continuity axioms for those functions.

More specifically, if we can pull out majora from
F
without discharging any further premises, this essentially means that one can pull the supremum of any
unordered
non-em
p
t
y countable set
S
out from
F
, i.e.
F(\sup S)=\sup F(S)
.
We would then postulate for such functions the axiom
c-cont
shown above right.
Dually, we would postulate
c-cocont
for functions that preserve countable infima.

\frac{a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752pt{\medcirc}\,a}{F\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752pt{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F\,a}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$\omega$-cont}}}

\frac{{\medcirc}\,a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752pta}{{{\raisebox{-0.69296pt}{$\square$}}}\,F\,a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752ptF\,{{\raisebox{-0.69296pt}{$\square$}}}\,a}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$\omega$-cocont}}}

A more commonly used notion of continuity, called
\omega
-continuity

[
1
]
, is when one need not be able to pull out suprema of any unordered set but only of ascending chains.
In AIC, this corresponds to being able to pull out majora of ascending chains and we would capture
\omega
-continuity of
F
by postulating
\omega
-cont
.
As with monotonicity, one can prove that
F
is c- or
\omega
-(co)continuous if and only if the appropriate quasi-inequalities are valid for sequence algebras, see
\Cref
sec:proof:omegaCont,sec:proof:alephCont.
Notions and axioms for minora and cocontinuity are entirely dual.

4
Algebraic Proofs of Fixed Point Theorems

To demonstrate the usefulness of AIC, we will now present purely algebraic proofs of well-known, less-well-known, and novel fixed point theorems.

4.1
Kleene, Tarski-Kantorovich, and Park

4.1.1
The Tarski-Kantorovich Principle

Before we prove in AIC the well-known
Kleene fixed point theorem
6
6

6

Though for our restricted setting where the lattice is countably complete.

[
28
]
, we consider a slightly more general result – the
Tarski-Kantorovich principle
(TKP)
[
21
]
.
It says: for
\omega
-continuous
F
, if
\ell\sqsubseteq F(\ell)
then iterating
F
on
\ell
will converge to the least fixed point of
F
just above

\ell
, which we denote by
\textnormal{{{lfp}}}_{{\sqsupseteq}\ell}~F
:

Theorem 14

(Tarski-Kantorovich Principle)
.

Let
F\colon L\to L
be an
\omega
-continuous endomap on a complete lattice
L
, and
\ell\in L
.
Then

\displaystyle\ell~{}\sqsubseteq{}~F(\ell)\hskip 20.00003pt\textnormal{implies}\hskip 20.00003pt\textnormal{{{lfp}}}_{{\sqsupseteq}\ell}~F~{}={}~\sup_{n\in\omega}\,F^{n}(\ell).

(TKP)

Remark 15

(On Duals)
.

TKP and all other fixed point theorems in this section have duals, which emerge by reversing the order (e.g. replace least with greatest fixed points,
\omega
-continuity with
\omega
-cocontinuity, etc.), which can be proved in AIC in the same way.
\lipicsEnd

AIC Formalization of the Tarski-Kantorovich Principle (
\Cref
thm:tkp)

Before we
algebraically prove
the theorem, we will first describe how to
formalize
it in AIC.
Let us assume some
a
such that
a\preceq F\,a
and that
\omega
-cont
holds for
F
.
While
a
is by default any sequence in AIC,
a
morally takes the role of the single lattice element
\ell
.
It would thus make sense to require that
a
is flat, so as to emulate a single lattice element.
However, it turns out that this is not necessary, as long as
a
is ascending, i.e.
a\preceq{\medcirc}\,a
.
Note that this makes the theorem we will prove more general.
To obtain the classical single-element version of
\Cref
thm:tkp, we would instantiate
a
with the flat sequence
\overline{\ell}=\ell~\ell~\ell~\ell\ldots{}
and that sequence is trivially ascending, hence
\overline{\ell}\preceq{\medcirc}\,\overline{\ell}
.

\frac{\textnormal{\footnotesize{{\color[rgb]{0.625,0.625,0.625}$\lbag$$F$\,~{$\omega$-cont}$\rbag$}}}\hskip 8.04996pta\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752pt{\medcirc}\,a\hskip 8.04996pta\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752ptF\,a}{F\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a\hskip 2.81752pt{}={}\hskip 2.81752pt{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}tkp-fp}}}

\frac{a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752ptF\,a}{a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752pt{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}tkp-above}}}

\frac{a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752ptb\hskip 8.04996ptF\,b\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752ptb\hskip 8.04996pt{\medcirc}\,b\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752ptb}{{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752ptb}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}tkp-least}}}

Assuming
a\preceq F\,a
and
a\preceq{\medcirc}\,a
, we formalize that
{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a
(cf.
\sup_{n\in\omega}F^{n}(\ell)
) is the least fixed point of
F\,
above
a
.
This comprises proving that (i)
{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a
is a fixed point, i.e. that
tkp-fp
shown above right
is derivable.
By
\lbag
…
\rbag
, we
annotate
which continuity axioms we need to postulate on
F
(cf.
\Cref
sec:calc,
Continuous Functions
) to prove the rule derivable.
This is just for convenience and bears no
further formal meaning
.

We further formalize that
{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a
is the
least
fixed point
above

a
, i.e. (ii)
{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a
is itself above
a
and (iii) any fixed point (and in particular any
pre
fixed point)
b
above
a
is also above
{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a
.
We thus derive the two quasi-inequalities
tkp-above
and
tkp-least
above.

Algebraic Proof of the Tarski-Kantorovich Principle (
\Cref
thm:tkp)

\frac{a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752pt{\medcirc}\,a}{{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F\,F^{*}a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752pt{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}${\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}$-quasi-pre-fp}}}

\frac{a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752ptF\,a}{{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752pt{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F\,F^{*}a}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}${\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}$-quasi-post-fp}}}

For proving
tkp-fp
,
it is useful to first prove that
F^{*}a
is a
majoral quasi fixed point
, i.e.
under the scope of a majorum
, it makes no
difference whether or not
F
is applied once more to
F^{*}a
; formally,

{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F\,F^{*}a\hskip 3.50006pt{}={}\hskip 3.50006pt{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a
, which intuitively means that
{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a
is a fixed point of
F

up to continuity
.
We divide this identity into the two quasi-inequalities shown in the box above right, and will later reuse these lemmas to prove the Olszewski fixed point theorem.
For a proof of
\Diamond
-quasi-pre-fp
, consider below the proof tree on the left; for
\Diamond
-quasi-post-fp
the one on the right.

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{\textnormal{\footnotesize{\raisebox{-1.72221pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}{\color[rgb]{0.435,0.655,0.875}{asm.}}}}}}}{a\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503pt{\medcirc}\,a}}{F\,F^{*}a\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503pt{\medcirc}\,F^{*}a}\enskip\textsc{Right={\hskip 2.25pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}asc-iter}}}}{{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F\,F^{*}a\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503pt{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,{\medcirc}\,F^{*}a}\enskip\textsc{Right={\hskip 2.25pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}${\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}$-mono}}}}{{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F\,F^{*}a\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503pt\hbox{\pagecolor{DodgerBlue3!30!white}${\medcirc}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a$}}\enskip\textsc{Right={\hskip 2.25pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}${\medcirc}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}$-comm}}}}{{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F\,F^{*}a\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503pt{\medcirc}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a~{}\curlyvee{}~F^{*}a}\hskip 8.04996pt\textsc{Right={\hskip 3.15pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$\curlyvee$-introR}}}}{{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F\,F^{*}a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752pt\hbox{\pagecolor{DodgerBlue3!30!white}$F^{*}a~{}\curlyvee{}~{\medcirc}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a$}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$\curlyvee$-comm}}}}{{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F\,F^{*}a\hskip 3.23753pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.23753pt\hbox{\pagecolor{DodgerBlue3!30!white}${\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a$}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}${\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}$-exp}}}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{\textnormal{\footnotesize{\raisebox{-1.72221pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}{\color[rgb]{0.435,0.655,0.875}{asm.}}}}}}}{a\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptF\,a}}{F^{*}a\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptF^{*}F\,a}\hskip 8.04996pt\textsc{Right={\hskip 3.15pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$F^{*}$-mono}}}}{F^{*}a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752pt\hbox{\pagecolor{DodgerBlue3!30!white}$F\,F^{*}a$}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$FF^{*}$-comm}}}}{{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a\hskip 3.23753pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.23753pt{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F\,F^{*}a}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}${\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}$-mono}}}

Before we show that
{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a
is a
proper
fixed point, we would like to point out the seemingly mundane essence of the two proofs shown above:

Both are about compensating for
one
excess
F
.

For
\Diamond
-quasi-pre-fp
(left tree), the excess
F
is on the left-hand side of the inequality.
To compensate, we can create an additional
F
on the right by expanding
\Diamond
, crucially: forgetting the irrelevant
F^{*}a
, creating a
{\medcirc}
on the non-forgotten part, and this
{\medcirc}
eventually (further up) acts on
F^{*}
to create an additional
F
(via
iter
hidden in
asc-iter
).

For
\Diamond
-quasi-post-fp
(right tree), the excess
F
is on the right, and while we
could
expand the left
\Diamond
, we cannot forget joinees (
\curlyvee
) on the left.
Therefore, instead of creating an
F
on the left, we need that the excess
F
on the right makes that side bigger which is essentially ensured by
a\preceq F\,a
.
As a precursor to the Olszewski theorem about
{{\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\square$}}}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a
we will see later:

a\preceq F\,a
would not have been necessary if we had some additional
\square
guarding
F^{*}a
.
Then we can expand
\square
on the left and forget the unshifted part of that
\square
-expansion.

We now show that

{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a
is a
proper
fixed point:
For
{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a
being a
post
fixed point (
tkp-post-fp
), we require only semi-continuity (which all monotonic functions satisfy):

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{\frac{\textnormal{\footnotesize{\raisebox{-1.72221pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}{\color[rgb]{0.435,0.655,0.875}{asm.}}}}}}}{a\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptF\,a}}{{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752pt{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F\,F^{*}a}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}${\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}$-quasi-post-fp}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{\textnormal{\footnotesize{\raisebox{-1.72221pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}semi-cont}}}}}{{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F\,F^{*}a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752ptF\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a}}{{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a\hskip 3.23753pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.23753ptF\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}trans}}}

For
{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a
being a
pre
fixed point, we require
“full”
continuity.
For
\omega
-continuous
F
, we have:

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{\textnormal{\footnotesize{\raisebox{-1.72221pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}{\color[rgb]{0.435,0.655,0.875}{asm.}}}}}}}{a\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptF\,a}}{F^{*}a\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503pt{\medcirc}\,F^{*}a}\hskip 8.04996pt\textsc{Right={\hskip 3.15pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}orbit-asc}}}}{F\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752pt{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F\,F^{*}a}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$\omega$-cont}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{\frac{\textnormal{\footnotesize{\raisebox{-1.72221pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}{\color[rgb]{0.435,0.655,0.875}{asm.}}}}}}}{a\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503pt{\medcirc}\,a}}{{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F\,F^{*}a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752pt{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}${\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}$-quasi-pre-fp}}}}{F\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a\hskip 3.23753pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.23753pt{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}trans}}}

For c-continuous
F
, the left branch of the above tree simplifies to:

\displaystyle\frac{\textnormal{\footnotesize{\raisebox{-1.72221pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}c-cont}}}}}{F\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a\hskip 3.23753pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.23753pt{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F\,F^{*}a}

Remark 16

(Prefixed Points of c-continuous Maps)
.

As a by-product, we have shown for ascending
a
that
{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a
is a prefixed point of any c-continuous map
F
.
Indeed,
a\preceq F\,a
is
not
required for being a prefixed point.
In the classical single-lattice-element setting this reads:
If
F
is c-continuous, then
\sup_{n\in\omega}F^{n}(\ell)
is a prefixed point of
F
for any
\ell\in L
.
While a minor result, this is to the best of our knowledge a novel result.
\lipicsEnd

We can now combine
{{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a}\preceq{F\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a}
and
{F\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a}\preceq{{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a}
using
antisymm
to obtain the desired quasiequation
tkp-fp
.
It is then left to prove
tkp-above
and
tkp-least
.
For
tkp-above
, see the proof tree below left.
For
tkp-least
, see the tree below right, thus completing the algebraic proof of
\Cref
thm:tkp.

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{\frac{\textnormal{\footnotesize{\raisebox{-1.72221pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}{\color[rgb]{0.435,0.655,0.875}{asm.}}}}}}}{a\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptF\,a}}{a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752ptF^{*}a}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$F$-ind}}}}{a\hskip 3.23753pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.23753pt{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}${\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}$-introR}}}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{\frac{\frac{\textnormal{\footnotesize{\raisebox{-1.72221pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}{\color[rgb]{0.435,0.655,0.875}{asm.}}}}}}}{a\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptb}\hskip 8.04996pt\frac{\textnormal{\footnotesize{\raisebox{-1.72221pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}{\color[rgb]{0.435,0.655,0.875}{asm.}}}}}}}{F\,b\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptb}}{F^{*}a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752ptb}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Left={{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$F^{*}$-introL}\hskip 4.5pt}}\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{\textnormal{\footnotesize{\raisebox{-1.72221pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}{\color[rgb]{0.435,0.655,0.875}{asm.}}}}}}}{{\medcirc}\,b\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752ptb}}{{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a\hskip 3.23753pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.23753ptb}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}${\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}$-introL}}}

∎

4.1.2
The Kleene Fixed Point Theorem

We obtain the Kleene fixed point theorem as an immediate corollary of the Tarski-Kantorovich principle, namely by instantiating
a=\bot
to obtain the very least fixed point (i.e. the least one above
\bot
), then given by
{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}\bot
(cf.
\sup_{n}F^{n}(\bot)
).

We can also easily read this corollary off the AIC proof of TKP.
This is because wherever
a\preceq{\medcirc}\,a
or
a\preceq F\,a
occur as assumptions in our proofs and we have
a=\bot
, we can vacuously discharge these assumptions by applying the axiom
bot
.

4.1.3
Park Induction

Similarly to the case for the Kleene fixed point theorem, the Park induction principle
[
34
]
for upper-bounding least fixed points of continuous functions
F
, i.e. for all
\ell\in L
,

F(\ell)~{}\sqsubseteq{}~\ell\hskip 20.00003pt\textnormal{implies}\hskip 20.00003pt\textnormal{{{lfp}}}~F~{}\sqsubseteq{}~\ell

is an immediate instance of
tkp-fp
,
tkp-above
,
tkp-least
for
a=\bot
and
b=\ell~\ell~\ell~\ell\ldots{}
. In fact, we can readily read off our algebraic proofs a
generalized
Park induction principle:

Theorem 17

(Generalized Park Induction)
.

For
\ell,m\in L
and
\omega
-continuous
F\colon L\to L
,

\displaystyle m~{}\sqsubseteq{}~F(m)\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{and}\hskip 10.00002ptm~{}\sqsubseteq{}~\ell\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{and}\hskip 10.00002ptF(\ell)~{}\sqsubseteq{}~\ell\hskip 20.00003pt\textnormal{implies}\hskip 20.00003pt\textnormal{{{lfp}}}_{{\sqsupseteq}m}~F~{}\sqsubseteq{}~\ell~.

Proof.

Instantiate
tkp-fp
and
tkp-above
with
a=m~m~m~m\ldots{}
and
b=\ell~\ell~\ell~\ell\ldots{}
(both flat).
It follows that
\llbracket{{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a}\rrbracket_{0}=\textnormal{{{lfp}}}_{{\sqsupseteq}m}~F
, and thus
\textnormal{{{lfp}}}_{{\sqsupseteq}m}~F\sqsubseteq\ell
by
tkp-least
.
∎

*Digression: Pen-and-Paper Algebraic Proofs of Fixed Point Theorems

Although the quasi-equational proof trees above are rigorous and readily automatable, they are not the way a human would typically approach a fixed point theorem.
For AIC to feel natural and genuinely useful, it should also facilitate the kind of compact and intuition-guided pen-and-paper-style arguments by which humans discover and prove such results.
Inspired by
[
36
, Figure 1]
, the example above illustrates this second mode of reasoning.
It showcases a pen-and-paper proof of the inequation
F\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}\bot\preceq{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}\bot
, i.e. the
“more difficult direction”
in showing that
\sup_{n}F^{n}(\bot)
is a fixed point of
F
as part of the Kleene fixed point theorem.
This is the kind of reasoning we ultimately want AIC to enable:
not only proofs that a theorem prover can check, but proofs that a human can actually read, understand, and internalize as a standard fixed point argument.

4.2
Olszewski

By the Tarski-Kantorovich principle (
Theorem 14
), seeding the iteration of an
\omega
-(co)continuous map
F
with a pre- or postfixed point of
F
converges to a
proper
fixed point.
But how to find pre- or postfixed points to begin with?
A recent result due to Olszewski shows that they emerge by iterating on an
arbitrary
seed element.

Theorem 18

(Olszewski
[
33
]
)
.

Let
F\colon L\to L
be
\omega
-cocontinuous and
\ell\in L
.
Then

\inf_{n\in\omega}~\sup_{k\geq n}~F^{k}(\ell)
is a p
ost
fixed point of
F
.

For Olszewski’s proof, see
Appendix
F.1
. Under stronger continuity assumptions on
F
, we can improve on Olszewski’s result and obtain
proper
fixed points:

Theorem 19

(Proper Olszewski Fixed Point)
.

Let
F\colon L\to L
be
\omega
-cocontinuous and c-continuous and
\ell\in L
.
Then

\inf_{n\in\omega}\sup_{k\geq n}F^{k}(\ell)
is a fixed point of
F
.

Remark 20
.

There is also a transfinite version of
Theorem 18
that only assumes monotonicity of
F
, see
[
32
, Thm. 1]
. Similarly to the transfinite version of the Knaster-Tarski Theorem by Cousot & Cousot
[
15
]
, this result is currently out of scope of AIC.

\lipicsEnd

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{\frac{\textnormal{\tiny{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}{\color[rgb]{0.435,0.655,0.875}{asm.}}}}}}}{{\medcirc}\,a\hskip 1.22502pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.22502pta}}{{{\raisebox{-0.44098pt}{$\square$}}}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a\hskip 1.71503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.71503pt{{\raisebox{-0.44098pt}{$\square$}}}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F\,F^{*}a}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Left={{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}${{\raisebox{-0.62997pt}{$\square$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}$-quasi-post-fp}\hskip 3.5pt}}\hskip 1.22502ptF\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a}}{{{\raisebox{-0.44098pt}{$\square$}}}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F\,F^{*}a\hskip 1.71503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.8681ptF\,{{\raisebox{-0.71747pt}{$\square$}}}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Right={\hskip 3.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}trans${}^{*}$}}}

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{\textnormal{\tiny{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}semi-cocont}}}}}{F\,{{\raisebox{-0.44098pt}{$\square$}}}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a\hskip 1.71503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.71503pt{{\raisebox{-0.44098pt}{$\square$}}}\,F\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a}\hskip 8.19447pt\frac{\frac{\textnormal{\tiny{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}c-cont}}}}}{F\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a\hskip 1.22502pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.22502pt{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F\,F^{*}a}}{{{\raisebox{-0.44098pt}{$\square$}}}\,F\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a\hskip 1.71503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.71503pt{{\raisebox{-0.44098pt}{$\square$}}}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F\,F^{*}a}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{right={\hskip 3.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}${{\raisebox{-0.62997pt}{$\square$}}}$-mono}}}\hskip 8.19447pt\frac{\frac{\textnormal{\tiny{\raisebox{-1.07639pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}{\color[rgb]{0.435,0.655,0.875}{asm.}}}}}}}{a\hskip 1.22502pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.22502pt{\medcirc}\,a}}{{{\raisebox{-0.44098pt}{$\square$}}}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F\,F^{*}a\hskip 1.71503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.71503pt{{\raisebox{-0.44098pt}{$\square$}}}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Right={\hskip 3.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}${{\raisebox{-0.62997pt}{$\square$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}$-quasi-pre-fp}}}}{F\,{{\raisebox{-0.71747pt}{$\square$}}}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a\hskip 2.8681pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.8681pt{{\raisebox{-0.71747pt}{$\square$}}}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Right={\hskip 3.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}trans${}^{*}$}}}

Figure 4:
Proof of
ol-post-fp
(top tree) and
ol-pre-fp
(bottom tree).

AIC Formalization of Olszewski Fixed Points (
\Cref
thm:olszewski,cor)

\frac{\textnormal{\footnotesize{{\color[rgb]{0.625,0.625,0.625}$\lbag$$F\,$ {$\omega$-cocont}$\rbag$}}}\hskip 8.04996pt{\medcirc}\,a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752pta}{{{\raisebox{-0.69296pt}{$\square$}}}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752ptF\,{{\raisebox{-0.69296pt}{$\square$}}}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}ol-post-fp}}}

\frac{\textnormal{\footnotesize{{\color[rgb]{0.625,0.625,0.625}$\lbag$$F\,$ {c-cont}$\rbag$}}}\hskip 8.04996pta\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752pt{\medcirc}\,a}{F\,{{\raisebox{-0.69296pt}{$\square$}}}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752pt{{\raisebox{-0.69296pt}{$\square$}}}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}ol-pre-fp}}}

\frac{\textnormal{\footnotesize{{\color[rgb]{0.625,0.625,0.625}$\lbag$$F\,$ {$\omega$-cocont}, $F\,$ {c-cont}$\rbag$}}}\hskip 8.04996pt{\medcirc}\,a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752pta\hskip 8.04996pta\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752pt{\medcirc}\,a}{{{\raisebox{-0.69296pt}{$\square$}}}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a\hskip 2.81752pt{}={}\hskip 2.81752ptF\,{{\raisebox{-0.69296pt}{$\square$}}}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}ol-fp}}}

As in
\Cref
sec:case_studies:kleene, the fixed seed element
\ell
is associated with a variable
a
.

\Cref
thm:olszewski then reads in AIC as
ol-post-fp
,
where – analogously to the AIC formalization of
\Cref
thm:tkp – we prove a more general statement and hence have an additional premise
{\medcirc}\,a\preceq a
.
\Cref
thm:olszewski is obtained from instantiating
a
with the flat sequence
\ell~\ell~\ell~\ell\ldots{}
, for which this premise holds vacuously.
Similarly,
\Cref
cor is formalized as
ol-fp
.

Algebraic Proof of Olszewski Fixed Points (
\Cref
thm:olszewski,cor)

\frac{{\medcirc}\,a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752pta}{{{\raisebox{-0.69296pt}{$\square$}}}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752pt{{\raisebox{-0.69296pt}{$\square$}}}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F\,F^{*}a}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}${{\raisebox{-0.80997pt}{$\square$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}$-quasi-post-fp}}}

\frac{a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752pt{\medcirc}\,a}{{{\raisebox{-0.69296pt}{$\square$}}}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F\,F^{*}a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752pt{{\raisebox{-0.69296pt}{$\square$}}}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}${{\raisebox{-0.80997pt}{$\square$}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}$-quasi-pre-fp}}}

Similarly to the proof of
tkp-fp
, it is useful to first prove that
F^{*}a
is a
mino-majoral quasi fixed point of
F\,
, i.e.
under the scope of a nested minorum-majorum
, it makes no difference whether or not
F
is applied once more to
F^{*}a
; formally,
{{\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\square$}}}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F\,F^{*}a\hskip 3.50006pt{}={}\hskip 3.50006pt{{\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\square$}}}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a
.
Thus, we derive the two rules
\square
\Diamond
-quasi-post-fp
and
\square
\Diamond
-quasi-pre-fp
above.

For
\square
\Diamond
-quasi-post-fp
, consider below the left proof tree.
Here, we can expand a
\square
on the left, which we did not have available for proving
\Diamond
-quasi-post-fp
.
This is the algebraic essence of why Olszewski will not require
a\preceq F\,a
or alike.

For
\square
\Diamond
-quasi-pre-fp
, we have the right proof tree below.
Notice that we re-used the
\Diamond
-quasi-pre-fp
rule from the proof of TKP.
Using
\square
\Diamond
-quasi-post-fp
, we prove
ol-post-fp
in
\Cref
fig:ol-postfp-proof (top).
Finally, with
ol-post-fp
at hand, the derivation of
ol-fp
follows immediately from
ol-pre-fp
which we derive also in
\Cref
fig:ol-postfp-proof (bottom),
thus concluding our algebraic proofs of
\Cref
thm:olszewski and
\Cref
cor.

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{{\medcirc}\,a\hskip 1.40002pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.40002pta}{{\medcirc}\,F^{*}a\hskip 1.40002pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.40002ptF\,F^{*}a}\hskip 4.0pt\textsc{Right={\hskip 2.0pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}desc-iter}}}}{{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,{\medcirc}\,F^{*}a\hskip 1.40002pt{}\preceq{}\hskip ∎

4.3
Latticed
\boldsymbol{k}
-Induction

Latticed
k
-induction
[
6
]
is a fixed point-theoretic generalization of the prominent
k
-induction verification principle
[
38
]
for hard- and software model checking
[
16
,
10
,
22
,
19
]
.
Latticed
k
-induction has successfully been employed for the verification of probabilistic programs
[
6
,
43
]
and probabilistic pushdown systems
[
42
]
.

Fix an
\omega
-continuous
F\colon L\to L
and an element
\ell\in L
. We call

G_{\ell}\colon L\to L
defined as
G_{\ell}(m)=F(m)\sqcap\ell

the
k
-Induction operator
.
The principle of
k
-induction is a generalization of Park induction:

Theorem 21

(Latticed
k
-Induction
[
6
]
)
.

For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle F\Bigl(G_{\ell}^{k}(\ell)\Bigr)~{}\sqsubseteq{}~\ell\hskip 20.00003pt\textnormal{implies}\hskip 20.00003pt\textnormal{{{lfp}}}~F~{}\sqsubseteq{}~\ell~.

AIC formalization of
\boldsymbol{k}
-Induction (
\Cref
thm:kind_prose)

Analogously to
\Cref
sec:case_studies:kleene, we associate the fixed lattice element
\ell
with a fixed
b
.
To formalize the
k
-fold application of the
k
-induction operator, we define for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
the term
G^{k}_{b}\,b
recursively by

G^{k}_{b}\,b~{}={}~\begin{cases}b&\text{if $k=0$}\\
F\,G^{k-1}_{b}\,b~~\curlywedge~~b&\text{otherwise}~.\end{cases}

The above is meant to define
s
y
ntactic
equality of terms, so e.g. defining that
G^{2}_{b}\,b
is a name for the term
F\,G^{1}_{b}\,b\curlywedge b
.

- .75

\frac{\textnormal{\footnotesize{{\color[rgb]{0.625,0.625,0.625}$\lbag$$F$\,~{$\omega$-cont}$\rbag$}}}\hskip 8.04996ptF\,G^{k}_{b}\,b\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752ptb\hskip 8.04996pt{\medcirc}\,b\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752ptb}{{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}\bot\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752ptb}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$k$-ind}}}

The algebraic version of
\Cref
thm:kind_prose then reads as a family of quasi-inequalities
k
-ind
(shown right, parametrized in
k
).
We require the additional premise
{\medcirc}\,b\hskip 3.50006pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.50006ptb
for the same reasons as in the formalization of
\Cref
thm:tkp.
Classical
k
-induction (
\Cref
thm:kind_prose) is obtained from

k
-ind
for
b=\ell~\ell~\ell~\ell\ldots{}
for which
{\medcirc}\,b\hskip 3.50006pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.50006ptb
holds vacuously. We prove
k
-ind
enitrely within
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{1}
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, constructing proofs inductively on
k
(cf.
Appendix
F.2
).

5
Mechanization

We mechanized AIC in Isabelle/HOL to confirm soundness and usability
[
9
]
.
The mechanization is a shallow embedding of sequence algebras on complete
lattices (cf.
\Cref
sec:synsem).
A shallow embedding means we do not
separately formalize the algebra as an object or define its syntax. Instead, we
directly build on Isabelle/HOL’s formalization of complete lattices and define
the operators of AIC as functions and constants in Isabelle. We use
functions from
\omega
to lattice elements as the type of sequences.
With this, the definition of operators is straightforward, and integrates so
well into the existing Isabelle lattice setup that for instance our
\curlywedge
and

\curlyvee
coincide with existing Isabelle type class operators
\sqcup
and

\sqcap

applied to functions
.

The axioms of AIC can then be derived as lemmas in Isabelle from the definition of the operators.
This builds up the algebra in
the prover and at the same time constitutes a soundness proof of
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{0}
as
well as any derived rules (e.g. the axioms in
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{1}\setminus{\mathsf{AIC}}_{0}
).

Using our formalization, we proved all fixed point
theorems of
\Cref
sec:case_studies.
The proofs follow
the paper presentation, with intermediate steps solved fully
automatically by Isabelle’s sledgehammer.
Beyond that, if the extended axiom system
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{1}
is available,

sledgehammer is even able to prove many high-level theorems such as
Tarski-Kantorovich or Park induction fully automatically
.
We also found that
Isabelle’s counterexample finder is effective in identifying misstated
lemmas. Both show promise for the use of the algebra and further exploration of
fixed point principles directly in the prover.
Conversely, if we remove
important derivable
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{1}
axioms such as
\Diamond
-exp
as explicit lemma statements and force the prover to work directly with expanded
definitions and indexed suprema and infima as in traditional fixed point
formalizations, automation tends to break down quickly.
Sledgehammer is able to
recover some lemmas by reproving them inline, but higher-level theorems remain
out of reach.
This means that
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{1}
brings with it not only increased clarity, but also
improves proof search
.

6
Completeness and Incompleteness

In this section, we investigate aspects of completeness of the axiom systems presented in
\Cref
sec:calc.
Completeness of
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{0}
means that if a finitary quasiequation is valid for sequence algebras, then it is derivable from
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{0}
.
In this section, we show that
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{0}
is
not
complete.
In fact, even if we restrict just to the
{\circleddash}\,
and
{\medcirc}\,
operations, no finite axiom system of finitary quasiequations can be complete.
This situation changes when allowing
infinitary
quasiequations and a slightly infinite notion of derivation, as we will see in
\Cref
sec:infinitary-ax.

6.1
Incompleteness of Finitary Axiomatizations

We now show that the validity of finitary quasiequations that just involve the
{\circleddash}
and
{\medcirc}
operations has no sound and complete finite axiomatization. To this end, it suffices to consider a simplified version of sequence algebras that drops all lattice-theoretic structure.

Definition 22

(Discrete Sequence Algebras)
.

A
discrete sequence algebra
is given by the set
A^{\omega}
of sequences in a set
A
equipped with the two unary operations
{\circleddash},{\medcirc}\colon A^{\omega}\to A^{\omega}
defined exactly the same as in
\Cref
def:seq-algebras (
3
).

All syntactic concepts for sequence algebras (terms, quasiequations, finite derivations) carry over to discrete sequence algebras by restricting all definitions to
{\circleddash}
and
{\medcirc}
.
We speak of
discrete
terms, quasiequations, etc. for distinction.
For instance, a discrete quasiequation is a quasiequation whose terms use only
{\circleddash}
and
{\medcirc}
.

A discrete quasiequation is
valid for discrete sequence algebras
if it is satisfied by every discrete sequence algebra. A discrete axiom system
\mathsf{Ax}
(i.e. a set of discrete finitary quasiequations) is
sound
for discrete sequence algebras if every quasiequation from
\mathsf{Ax}
is valid for discrete sequence algebras. It is
complete
for discrete sequence algebras if every quasiequation that is valid for discrete sequence algebras has a finite derivation from
\mathsf{Ax}
.

Theorem 23

(Incompleteness)
.

There is no finite discrete axiom system that is both sound and complete for discrete sequence algebras.

Intuitively, the reason is that a finite discrete axiom system cannot derive non-trivial statements about periodic sequences with large periods, since the finitely many finitary axioms necessarily only contain statements about periods of bounded length. Specifically, for each positive integer
N
, consider the discrete quasiequation

{\medcirc}^{N}\,a~{}={}~a\hskip 10.00002pt{\wedge}\hskip 10.00002pt\bigwedge_{i=0}^{N-1}~{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}^{i}\,a~{}={}~{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}^{i+1}\,a\hskip 10.00002pt{}\Longrightarrow{}\hskip 10.00002pt{\medcirc}\,a~{}={}~a

(6)

stating that if a sequence is periodic with period length
N
and its first
N
elements are equal, then the sequence is flat.
While (
6
) is valid for discrete sequence algebras, one can show that for every finite and sound discrete axiom system
\mathsf{Ax}
, this quasiequation is not derivable from
\mathsf{Ax}
for sufficiently large
N
.
Thus
\mathsf{Ax}
is not complete.

Despite the simple underlying idea, the proof of
\Cref
thm:incompleteness is fairly involved; it rests on the introduction of a suitable normal form for quasiequations and a careful analysis of possible derivations from normalized quasiequations.
For details, see
Appendix
G
.

6.2
A Complete Infinitary Axiomatization

As we have just seen, no finite set of finitary quasiequations can completely capture validity.
For achieving completeness, we need to add possibly
infinitary
quasiequations, i.e.
(\bigwedge_{i\in I}{{}^{i}}s={{}^{i}}t)\Longrightarrow s=t
where
I
may be infinite.
As the size of
I
determines the branching degree in derivation trees, we can no longer be satisfied with our finite derivation trees (cf.
\Cref
def:finite provability).
It turns out that a slightly different kind of finiteness provides just the right notion of derivation for infinitary quasiequational logic.

Definition 24

(Well-founded Derivation)
.

An inference tree is
well-founded
if every branch of the tree has finite height.
Given an axiom system
\mathsf{Ax}
and an quasiequation
q
, a
well-founded
derivation of
q
from
\mathsf{Ax}
is defined exactly as in
\Cref
def:finite provability, except that the underlying inference tree is well-founded in lieu of finite.
A quasiequation
q
is
well-foundedly derivable
from
\mathsf{Ax}
, denoted
\mathsf{Ax}\vdash_{\mathsf{wf}}\textup{{q}}
, if
q
has a well-founded derivation from
\mathsf{Ax}
.

By Kőnig’s lemma, a finitely branching well-founded tree is finite.
Thus our previous notion of finite derivability (
\Cref
def:finite provability) coincides with well-founded derivability when all of the quasiequations from
\mathsf{Ax}
are finitary.
In particular, since all
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{0}
axioms are finitary, a quasiequation
q
is derivable from
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{0}
if and only if
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{0}\vdash_{\mathsf{wf}}\textup{{q}}
.

Importantly, we also cannot be satisfied with inference trees of finite height, even with infinitary branching.
Indeed,
\mathsf{Ax}
could contain
(\bigwedge_{i\in I}{{}^{i}}s={{}^{i}}t)\Longrightarrow s=t
with
I=\omega
and each identity
{{}^{i}}s={{}^{i}}t
could require a derivation of height
i
, like so:

\frac{\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.625,0.625,0.625}\text{\scriptsize(derivation height 0)}}}{{{}^{0}}s={{}^{0}}t}\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.625,0.625,0.625}\text{\scriptsize(derivation height 1)}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.625,0.625,0.625}\cdots}}}{{{}^{1}}s={{}^{1}}t}\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{\frac{\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.625,0.625,0.625}\text{\scriptsize(derivation height 2)}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.625,0.625,0.625}\cdots}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.625,0.625,0.625}\cdots}}}{{{}^{2}}s={{}^{2}}t}\hskip 9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0.625,0.625,0.625}\cdots}}{s=t}

This is a derivation tree of infinite height, but it also well-founded, because every branch has finite height.
To summarize, well-founded derivations are sufficient and (in general) necessary for reasoning with infinitary inference rules.
This is the content of the theorem below, which appears to be folklore.
7
7

7

For the sake of convenience, a proof can be found in the appendix.

We state the theorem for AIC-structures, but it extends to algebras over any finitary signature.

Theorem 25

(Soundness and Completeness of Well-founded Derivations)
.

Let
\mathsf{Ax}
be an axiom system and let
q
be a quasiequation.
Then
\mathsf{Ax}\vdash_{\mathsf{wf}}\textup{{q}}
if and only if for any AIC-structure
{\mathcal{A}}\models\mathsf{Ax}
, we also have
{\mathcal{A}}\models\textup{{q}}
.

Lattice

\begin{gathered}\bot~{}\preceq{}~a\hskip 18.49988pta~{}\preceq{}~\top\hskip 18.49988pta\curlyvee b~{}={}~b\curlyvee a\hskip 18.49988pta\curlywedge b~{}={}~b\curlywedge a\\
a\curlywedge(a\curlyvee b)~{}={}~a~{}={}~a\curlyvee(a\curlywedge b)\hskip 18.49988pta~{}\preceq{}~b~{}\Longrightarrow{}~Fa~{}\preceq{}~Fb\\
a\curlywedge(b\curlywedge c)~{}={}~(a\curlywedge b)\curlywedge c\hskip 18.49988pta\curlyvee(b\curlyvee c)~{}={}~(a\curlyvee b)\curlyvee c\\
\end{gathered}

Heads

\begin{gathered}\hskip-11.56242pt{\circleddash}\,\bot~{}={}~\bot\hskip 18.49988pt{\circleddash}\,\top~{}={}~\top\hskip 18.49988pt{\circleddash}\,(a\curlywedge b)~{}={}~{\circleddash}\,a\curlywedge{\circleddash}\,b\hskip 18.49988pt{\circleddash}\,(a\curlyvee b)~{}={}~{\circleddash}\,a\curlyvee{\circleddash}\,b\\
\hskip-11.56242pt{\circleddash}\,Fa~{}={}~F{\circleddash}\,a\hskip 18.49988pt{\circleddash}\,F^{*}a~{}={}~{\medcirc}\,{\circleddash}\,a~{}={}~{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,{\circleddash}\,a~{}={}~{{\raisebox{-0.83246pt}{$\square$}}}\,{\circleddash}\,a~{}={}~{\circleddash}\,{\circleddash}\,a~{}={}~{\circleddash}\,a\end{gathered}

Shifts

\begin{gathered}\hskip-11.56242pt{\medcirc}\,\bot~{}={}~\bot\hskip 18.49988pt{\medcirc}\,\top~{}={}~\top\hskip 18.49988pt{\medcirc}\,(a\curlyvee b)~{}={}~{\medcirc}\,a\curlyvee{\medcirc}\,b\hskip 18.49988pt{\medcirc}\,(a\curlywedge b)~{}={}~{\medcirc}\,a\curlywedge{\medcirc}\,b\\
\hskip-11.56242pt{\medcirc}\,Fa~{}={}~F{\medcirc}\,a\hskip 18.49988pt{\medcirc}\,F^{*}a~{}={}~FF^{*}{\medcirc}\,a\hskip 18.49988pt{\medcirc}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a~{}={}~{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,{\medcirc}\,a\hskip 18.49988pt{\medcirc}\,{{\raisebox{-0.83246pt}{$\square$}}}\,a~{}={}~{{\raisebox{-0.83246pt}{$\square$}}}\,{\medcirc}\,a\end{gathered}

Majora &

Minora

\begin{gathered}a~{}\preceq{}~{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a\hskip 18.49988pt{{\raisebox{-0.83246pt}{$\square$}}}\,a~{}\preceq{}~a\hskip 18.49988pt{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a~{}={}~{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a\hskip 18.49988pt{{\raisebox{-0.83246pt}{$\square$}}}\,{{\raisebox{-0.83246pt}{$\square$}}}\,a~{}={}~{{\raisebox{-0.83246pt}{$\square$}}}\,a\\
{\medcirc}\,a~{}\preceq{}~a~{}\Longleftrightarrow{}~{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a~{}\preceq{}~a\hskip 18.49988pta~{}\preceq{}~{\medcirc}\,a~{}\Longleftrightarrow{}~a~{}\preceq{}~{{\raisebox{-0.83246pt}{$\square$}}}\,a\end{gathered}

Sequence

\begin{gathered}\bigwedge_{n\in\omega}{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{n}a~{}\preceq{}~b~{}\Longrightarrow{}~{\circleddash}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a~{}\preceq{}~b\hskip 18.49988pt\bigwedge_{n\in\omega}b~{}\preceq{}~{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{n}a~{}\Longrightarrow{}~b~{}\preceq{}~{\circleddash}\,{{\raisebox{-0.83246pt}{$\square$}}}\,a\\
\bigwedge_{n\in\omega}{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{n}a~{}={}~{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{n}b~{}\Longrightarrow{}~a~{}={}~b\\
\end{gathered}

Figure 5:

The complete infinitary axiom system
{{\mathsf{AIC}}_{\omega}}
. Above,
{a,b,c\in\mathsf{Vars}}
are fixed variables.

table*

The axioms of
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{\omega}
. Below,
a,b,c\in\mathsf{Vars}
are fixed variables.

Lattice Axioms

\begin{gathered}\bot~{}\preceq{}~a\hskip 9.24994pta~{}\preceq{}~\top\hskip 9.24994pta\curlywedge(a\curlyvee b)=a=a\curlyvee(a\curlywedge b)\hskip 9.24994pt(a~{}\preceq{}~b)\Rightarrow(Fa~{}\preceq{}~Fb)\\
a\curlywedge(b\curlywedge c)=(a\curlywedge b)\curlywedge c\hskip 9.24994pta\curlyvee(b\curlyvee c)=(a\curlyvee b)\curlyvee c\\
a\curlyvee b=b\curlyvee a\hskip 9.24994pta\curlywedge b=b\curlywedge a\\
\end{gathered}

Head Axioms

\begin{gathered}{\circleddash}\,\bot=\bot\hskip 9.24994pt{\circleddash}\,\top=\top\hskip 9.24994pt{\circleddash}\,(a\curlywedge b)={\circleddash}\,a\curlywedge{\circleddash}\,b\hskip 9.24994pt{\circleddash}\,(a\curlyvee b)={\circleddash}\,a\curlyvee{\circleddash}\,b\\
{\circleddash}\,Fa=F{\circleddash}\,a\hskip 9.24994pt{\circleddash}\,F^{*}a={\medcirc}\,{\circleddash}\,a={\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,{\circleddash}\,a={{\raisebox{-0.83246pt}{$\square$}}}\,{\circleddash}\,a={\circleddash}\,{\circleddash}\,a={\circleddash}\,a\end{gathered}

Shift Axioms

\begin{gathered}{\medcirc}\,\bot=\bot\hskip 9.24994pt{\medcirc}\,\top=\top\hskip 9.24994pt{\medcirc}\,(a\curlyvee b)={\medcirc}\,a\curlyvee{\medcirc}\,b\hskip 9.24994pt{\medcirc}\,(a\curlywedge b)={\medcirc}\,a\curlywedge{\medcirc}\,b\\
{\medcirc}\,Fa=F{\medcirc}\,a\hskip 9.24994pt{\medcirc}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a={\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,{\medcirc}\,a\hskip 9.24994pt{\medcirc}\,{{\raisebox{-0.83246pt}{$\square$}}}\,a={{\raisebox{-0.83246pt}{$\square$}}}\,{\medcirc}\,a\hskip 9.24994pt{\medcirc}\,F^{*}a=FF^{*}{\medcirc}\,a\end{gathered}

Maj/Minorum

\begin{gathered}a~{}\preceq{}~{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a\hskip 9.24994pt{{\raisebox{-0.83246pt}{$\square$}}}\,a~{}\preceq{}~a\hskip 9.24994pt{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a={\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a\hskip 9.24994pt{{\raisebox{-0.83246pt}{$\square$}}}\,{{\raisebox{-0.83246pt}{$\square$}}}\,a={{\raisebox{-0.83246pt}{$\square$}}}\,a\\
({\medcirc}\,a~{}\preceq{}~a)\Leftrightarrow({\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a~{}\preceq{}~a)\hskip 9.24994pt(a~{}\preceq{}~{\medcirc}\,a)\Leftarrow(a~{}\preceq{}~{{\raisebox{-0.83246pt}{$\square$}}}\,a)\end{gathered}

Sequence Axioms

\begin{gathered}\textstyle(\bigwedge_{n\in\omega}b~{}\preceq{}~{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{n}a)\Rightarrow(b~{}\preceq{}~{\circleddash}\,{{\raisebox{-0.83246pt}{$\square$}}}\,a)\hskip 9.24994pt(\bigwedge_{n\in\omega}{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{n}a~{}\preceq{}~b)\Rightarrow({\circleddash}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a~{}\preceq{}~b)\\
\textstyle(\bigwedge_{n\in\omega}{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{n}a={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{n}b)\Rightarrow(a=b)\\
\end{gathered}

An
infinitary
axiom system
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{\omega}
that is sound and complete for sequence algebras is given in
\Cref
tab:complete AIC (for economy of space, we present the axioms as implications rather than inference rules).
Note that there are exactly three infinitary axioms, namely the sequence axioms. To understand these axioms, it helps to recall (cf.
\Cref
sec:sequence-algebras) that
– in
sequence
algebras –

{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{n}a
represents the
n^{\textnormal{th}}
element of a sequence
a
,
{\circleddash}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a
the supremum of
a
, and
{\circleddash}\,{{\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\square$}}}\,a
its infimum.
Intuitively, the first sequence axiom states that if every element of
a
is bounded above by the entirety of a sequence
b
(cf.
\bigwedge_{n\in\omega}{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{n}a\preceq b
), then the supremum of
a
is also bounded above by the entirety of
b
(cf.
{\circleddash}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a\preceq b
).
The second sequence axiom is the dual statement for infima.
The third sequence axiom captures that if two sequences agree at every index (cf.
\bigwedge_{n\in\omega}{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{n}a={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{n}b
), then the two sequences are equal (cf.
a=b
).
This is needed in the embedding theorem,
\cref
thm:embedding.

Theorem 26

(Soundness and Completeness of
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{\omega}
)
.

For any quasiequation
q
,

\text{${\mathsf{AIC}}_{\omega}\vdash_{\mathsf{wf}}\textup{{q}}$}\hskip 20.00003pt\textnormal{iff}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{$\textup{{q}}$ is valid for sequence algebras}.

The proof of
\Cref
thm:completeness uses two results:
The first one is
\Cref
thm:quasiequational completeness, which tells us that to show that a quasiequation is well-foundedly derivable in
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{\omega}
, it suffices to show that it is true in all models (not just sequence algebras) of
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{\omega}
.
The second result is the
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{\omega}
embedding theorem
below, which tells us that every AIC-structure that satisfies
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{\omega}
embeds into some sequence algebra.

Theorem 27

(
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{\omega}
-Embedding)
.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}\models{\mathsf{AIC}}_{\omega}
be an AIC-structure.
Then there exists a complete lattice
(L,\,{\sqsubseteq})
and a sequence algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
carried by
L^{\omega}
such that
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}

embeds
into
{\mathcal{A}}
, in the sense that there exists an injective algebra homomorphism
\varepsilon\colon{\mathcal{A}}_{0}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
.

Proof Sketch for
\Cref
thm:embedding.

Given an AIC-structure
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}\models{\mathsf{AIC}}_{\omega}
, we first need to find a complete lattice
L
, which later will carry the sequence algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
.
We start by defining a (possibly incomplete) lattice
L_{0}
as follows: let
A_{0}
carry
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
, and define
L_{0}=\{{\circleddash}^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathcal{A}}_{0}}}x\mid x\in A_{0}\}
.
Note that
L_{0}\subseteq A_{0}
.
From the first row of the
Heads
axioms in
\Cref
tab:complete AIC, we see that
L_{0}
is a lattice when equipped with the lattice operations of
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
: given
x,y\in A_{0}
, we define

\top^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}L_{0}}}~{}={}~{\circleddash}^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathcal{A}}_{0}}}\,\top^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathcal{A}}_{0}}}\hskip 20.00003pt\textnormal{and}\hskip 20.00003pt{\circleddash}^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathcal{A}}_{0}}}\,x~{}\sqcap^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}L_{0}}}{}~{\circleddash}^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathcal{A}}_{0}}}\,y~{}={}~{\circleddash}^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathcal{A}}_{0}}}\,(x\mathbin{{\curlywedge}\!{}^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathcal{A}}_{0}}}}y)

and similarly for
\bot{\!}^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}L_{0}}}
and
\sqcup^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}L_{0}}}
.
Furthermore, for each
F\in\mathsf{Funcs}
, the operation
F^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathcal{A}}_{0}}}
restricts to a monotone map
F^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}L_{0}}}\colon L_{0}\to L_{0}
defined by
F^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}L_{0}}}({\circleddash}\,^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathcal{A}}_{0}}}x)={\circleddash}\,^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathcal{A}}_{0}}}F^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathcal{A}}_{0}}}(x)

for each
x\in A_{0}
by the bottom-left equation in the
Heads
axioms of
\Cref
tab:complete AIC.

The only issue with stopping here and declaring that
L=L_{0}
is that
L_{0}
may be incomplete.
This is where we make use of a theorem of Macneille
[
29
]
, which states that every lattice
L_{0}
admits an order-embedding
into a complete lattice
L
called the
Dedekind-Macneille completion of
L_{0}
.
So, let
m\colon L_{0}\hookrightarrow L
be the Dedekind-Macneille completion of
L_{0}
.
Every monotone map
F_{0}\colon L_{0}\to L_{0}
extends to a (not necessarily unique) monotone map
F\colon L\to L
, in the sense that
F\circ m=m\circ F_{0}
; see
[
40
, Proposition 3.3]
.

Thus, we arrive at the sequence algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
and the injective algebra homomorphism
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
that we were looking for.
The sequence algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
is carried by
L^{\omega}
, where
L
is the Dedekind-Macneille completion of
L_{0}
, and for each function symbol
F\in\mathsf{Funcs}
, the operation
F^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathcal{A}}}}
is the one obtained from some extension of
F^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}L_{0}}}\colon L_{0}\to L_{0}
to a monotone map
F^{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}L}}\colon L\to L
(see
\Cref
def:seq-algebras).
The embedding
\varepsilon\colon A_{0}\to L^{\omega}
is then obtained by forming sequences using the head and shift operators: for any
x\in A_{0}
and
n\in\omega
, we set

\varepsilon(x)_{n}=m\big({\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{n}x\big)
.
Since
m
is an order-embedding, it follows from the last axiom in the
Sequence
axioms of
\Cref
tab:complete AIC that
\varepsilon
is injective.
Verifying that
\varepsilon
is an algebra homomorphism amounts to a long calculation; see
Appendix
H
for details.
∎

Proof of
\Cref
thm:completeness.

Soundness (
\Longrightarrow
) holds by
\Cref
thm:quasiequational completeness and the observation that all axioms in
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{\omega}
are valid for sequence algebras. For completeness (
\Longleftarrow
), consider any quasiequation
q
and
suppose that for any sequence model
{\mathcal{A}}
,
{\mathcal{A}}\models\textup{{q}}
.
We need to show that
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{\omega}\vdash_{\mathsf{wf}}\textup{{q}}
.
By
\Cref
thm:quasiequational completeness, it suffices to argue that
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}\models\textup{{q}}
for any AIC-structure
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
that satisfies
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{\omega}
.
But given any such
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
, by
\Cref
thm:embedding there is an injective algebra homomorphism
\varepsilon\colon{\mathcal{A}}_{0}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{A}}
into some sequence algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
.
Quasiequations are universally quantified, so whenever an algebra satisfies some quasiequation, so do all its subalgebras. Therefore, from our assumption that
{\mathcal{A}}\models\textup{{q}}
it follows that
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}\models\textup{{q}}
, as desired.
∎

One could also ask for an axiomatization of the class of sequence algebras itself: is there a set
\mathsf{Ax}
of quasiequations such that an AIC-structure satisfies
\mathsf{Ax}
iff it is a sequence algebra? Such an axiomatization does not exist: by the quasivariety theorem
[
13
, Thm. 2.25]
, any class of algebras with a quasiequational axiomatization is closed under subalgebras. But sequence algebras are not; e.g., every sequence algebra has the AIC-subalgebra given by all eventually constant sequences, which is generally not isomorphic to a sequence algebra.

7
Limitations and Outlook

AIC currently cannot reason about
transfinite
fixed point iterations.
If it could, this might enable reasoning about fixed points of
arbitrary monotonic
endomaps, e.g. by algebraizing the constructive version of the Knaster-Tarski theorem by Cousot & Cousot
[
15
]
.

As for a connection to modal logics, linear time temporal logic (
LTL
)
[
35
]
seems to be closely related to the sequence-part of AIC.
While the purpose of
LTL
is completely different and it does not have iteration modalities, it would still be interesting to investigate how strong the connection between
LTL
and AIC really is.
The fact that both AIC and
LTL
admit expansion laws as well as absorption laws a là
{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,{{\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\square$}}}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a={{\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\square$}}}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a
suggests a strong connection.

In verification, one could investigate whether Olszewski-type fixed points admit a temporal interpretation a là
“
{{\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\square$}}}\,{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}a
are the states that always eventually reach
a
by taking
F
-transitions”
.
Another endeavor in verification is to study whether Olszewski-type fixed points can help to better understand why techniques like
k
-induction
[
38
,
6
]
work rather well and how generalization in IC3
[
12
]
can be understood from a fixed point theoretic point of view in order to transfer this technique to more general settings
[
25
,
7
]
.

Another promising direction for future work is to study non-standard (i.e. non-sequence) models of AIC. From
\Cref
thm:completeness applied to the axiom system
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{0}
and the incompleteness result of
\Cref
sec:incompleteness, it follows that many such models exist. We leave it as future work to discover interesting examples of non-standard models. Particularly interesting would be models where the inhabitants can also have a sort of
“orientation”
or
“polarity”
à la ascending/descending.
Possible candidates for such structures would be formal languages, trees, directed sets, continuous real-valued functions, or permutations.

Appendix A
Appendix: Soundness of
\boldsymbol{{\mathsf{AIC}}_{0}}

In this section, we prove the soundness of the
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{0}
axiom system (see
\Cref
fig:axioms-aic0).

See
11

Proof.

For every axiom
\textup{{q}}\in{\mathsf{AIC}}_{0}

In the following, we go over every axiom in
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{0}
.
Assume that
n
is arbitrary but fixed.
Furthermore, we assume that the interpretation of the variables and function symbols
\mathfrak{I}
is also arbitrary but fixed and we will mostly drop the superscript

for readability.

\displaystyle\frac{\textnormal{\small{\raisebox{-1.93748pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}bot}}}}}{\bot\hskip 3.50006pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.50006pta}\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{\textnormal{\small{\raisebox{-1.93748pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}top}}}}}{a\hskip 3.50006pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.50006pt\top}

\Cref
fig:axioms-aic0,
Bounded Lattice

For the first few axioms, there are no premises. We only have to prove that the conclusion always holds.

\displaystyle\llbracket{\bot}\rrbracket_{n}

\displaystyle~{}={}~\bot

(by
\Cref
tab:semantics)

\displaystyle~{}\sqsubseteq{}~\llbracket{a}\rrbracket_{n}

(by
\bot
being the least element of the underlying lattice)

The proof of
top
is entirely dual by
\top
being the greatest element of the underlying lattice.

\displaystyle\frac{\textnormal{\small{\raisebox{-1.93748pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$\curlyvee$-comm}}}}}{a\curlyvee b\hskip 3.50006pt{}={}\hskip 3.50006ptb\curlyvee a}\hskip 20.00003pt\frac{\textnormal{\small{\raisebox{-1.93748pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$\curlywedge$-comm}}}}}{a\curlywedge b\hskip 3.50006pt{}={}\hskip 3.50006ptb\curlywedge a}

\Cref
fig:axioms-aic0,
Bounded Lattice

\displaystyle\llbracket{a\curlyvee b}\rrbracket_{n}

\displaystyle~{}={}~\llbracket{a}\rrbracket_{n}~{}\sqcup{}~\llbracket{b}\rrbracket_{n}

(by
\Cref
tab:semantics)

\displaystyle~{}={}~\llbracket{b}\rrbracket_{n}~{}\sqcup{}~\llbracket{a}\rrbracket_{n}

(by commutativity of
\sqcup
)

\displaystyle~{}={}~\llbracket{b\curlyvee a}\rrbracket_{n}

(by
\Cref
tab:semantics)

The proof of
\curlywedge
-comm
is entirely analogous by commutativity of
\sqcap
.

\displaystyle\frac{\textnormal{\small{\raisebox{-1.93748pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$\curlyvee$-absorb}}}}}{a~{}\curlyvee{}~(a\curlywedge b)\hskip 3.50006pt{}={}\hskip 3.50006pta}\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{\textnormal{\small{\raisebox{-1.93748pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$\curlywedge$-absorb}}}}}{a~{}\curlywedge{}~(a\curlyvee b)\hskip 3.50006pt{}={}\hskip 3.50006pta}

\Cref
fig:axioms-aic0,
Bounded Lattice

\displaystyle\llbracket{a\curlyvee(a\curlywedge b)}\rrbracket_{n}

\displaystyle~{}={}~\llbracket{a}\rrbracket_{n}~{}\sqcup{}~\bigl(\llbracket{a}\rrbracket_{n}\sqcap\llbracket{b}\rrbracket_{n}\bigr)

(by
\Cref
tab:semantics)

\displaystyle~{}={}~\llbracket{a}\rrbracket_{n}

(by absorption laws of complete lattices)

The proof of
\curlywedge
-absorb
is entirely dual, also by the absorptions laws of complete lattices.

\displaystyle\frac{\textnormal{\small{\raisebox{-1.93748pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$\curlyvee$-assoc}}}}}{(a\curlyvee b)~{}\curlyvee{}~c\hskip 3.50006pt{}={}\hskip 3.50006pta~{}\curlyvee{}~(b\curlyvee c)}\frac{\textnormal{\small{\raisebox{-1.93748pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$\curlywedge$-assoc}}}}}{(a\curlywedge b)~{}\curlywedge{}~c\hskip 3.50006pt{}={}\hskip 3.50006pta~{}\curlywedge{}~(b\curlywedge c)}

Fig.
2
,
Bound. Lat.

\displaystyle\llbracket{a\curlyvee(b\curlyvee c)}\rrbracket_{n}

\displaystyle~{}={}~\llbracket{a}\rrbracket_{n}~{}\sqcup{}~\bigl(\llbracket{b}\rrbracket_{n}~{}\sqcup{}~\llbracket{c}\rrbracket_{n}\bigr)

(by
\Cref
tab:semantics)

\displaystyle~{}={}~\bigl(\llbracket{a}\rrbracket_{n}~{}\sqcup{}~\llbracket{b}\rrbracket_{n}\bigr)~{}\sqcup{}~\llbracket{c}\rrbracket_{n}

(by associativity of
\sqcup
)

\displaystyle~{}={}~\llbracket{(a\curlyvee b)\curlyvee c}\rrbracket_{n}

(by
\Cref
tab:semantics)

The proof of
\curlywedge
-assoc
is entirely analogous by associativity of
\sqcap
.

\displaystyle\frac{a\hskip 3.50006pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.50006ptb}{{\medcirc}\,a\hskip 3.50006pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.50006pt{\medcirc}\,b}\hskip 10.00002pt\textsc{right={\hskip 5.0pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}${\medcirc}$-mono}}}

\Cref
fig:axioms-aic0,
Shifts

This axiom has a premise, namely
a\preceq b
.
We thus assume that

\displaystyle\llbracket{a}\rrbracket_{k}~{}\sqsubseteq{}~\llbracket{b}\rrbracket_{k}

(
asm.
)

holds for all
k\in\omega
, in particular for any
n,\,n+1\in\omega
.
Then consider the following:

\displaystyle\llbracket{{\medcirc}\,a}\rrbracket_{n}

\displaystyle~{}={}~\llbracket{a}\rrbracket_{n+1}

(by
\Cref
tab:semantics)

\displaystyle~{}\sqsubseteq{}~\llbracket{b}\rrbracket_{n+1}

(by
asm.
)

\displaystyle~{}={}~\llbracket{{\medcirc}\,b}\rrbracket_{n}

(by
\Cref
tab:semantics)

\displaystyle\frac{\textnormal{\small{\raisebox{-1.93748pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}${\medcirc}$-of-$\bot$}}}}}{{\medcirc}\,\bot\hskip 3.50006pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.50006pt\bot}{}\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{\textnormal{\small{\raisebox{-1.93748pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}${\medcirc}$-of-$\top$}}}}}{\top\hskip 3.50006pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.50006pt{\medcirc}\,\top}{}

\Cref
fig:axioms-aic0,
Shifts

\displaystyle\llbracket{{\medcirc}\,\bot}\rrbracket_{n}~{}={}~\llbracket{\bot}\rrbracket_{n+1}

\displaystyle~{}={}~\bot

(by
\Cref
tab:semantics)

\displaystyle~{}\sqsubseteq{}~\bot

(by reflexivity of
\sqsubseteq
)

\displaystyle~{}={}~\llbracket{\bot}\rrbracket_{n}

(by
\Cref
tab:semantics)

The proof of
{\medcirc}
-of-
\top
is entirely analogous.

\displaystyle\frac{\textnormal{\small{\raisebox{-1.93748pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}${\medcirc}$-over-$\curlyvee$}}}}}{{\medcirc}\,(a\curlyvee b)\hskip 3.50006pt{}={}\hskip 3.50006pt{\medcirc}\,a~{}\curlyvee{}~{\medcirc}\,b}\hskip 20.00003pt\frac{\textnormal{\small{\raisebox{-1.93748pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}${\medcirc}$-over-$\curlywedge$}}}}}{{\medcirc}\,(a\curlywedge b)\hskip 3.50006pt{}={}\hskip 3.50006pt{\medcirc}\,a~{}\curlywedge{}~{\medcirc}\,b}

\Cref

Shifts

\displaystyle\llbracket{{\medcirc}\,(a\curlyvee b)}\rrbracket_{n}~{}={}~\llbracket{a\curlyvee b}\rrbracket_{n+1}~{}={}~\llbracket{a}\rrbracket_{n+1}~{}\sqcup{}~\llbracket{b}\rrbracket_{n+1}~{}={}~\llbracket{{\medcirc}\,a}\rrbracket_{n}~{}\sqcup{}~\llbracket{{\medcirc}\,b}\rrbracket_{n}~{}={}~\llbracket{{\medcirc}\,a\curlyvee{\medcirc}\,b}\rrbracket_{n}

(all by

The proof of
{\medcirc}
-over-
