4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}${\medcirc}$-over-$\curlyvee$}}}}{{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,(a\curlyvee b)\hskip 3.23753pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.23753pt{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a\curlyvee{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,b}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}${\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}$-introL}}}

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{\textnormal{\footnotesize{\raisebox{-1.72221pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}reflex}}}}}{a\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503pta}}{a\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503pta\curlyvee b}\hskip 8.04996pt\textsc{Left={{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$\curlyvee$-introR}\hskip 3.15pt}}}{{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752pt{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,(a\curlyvee b)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Left={{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}${\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}$-mono}\hskip 4.5pt}}\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{\frac{\frac{\textnormal{\footnotesize{\raisebox{-1.72221pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}reflex}}}}}{b\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptb}}{b\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503pta\curlyvee b}\hskip 8.04996pt\textsc{Right={\hskip 3.15pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$\curlyvee$-introR}}}}{{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,b\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752pt{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,(a\curlyvee b)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}${\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}$-mono}}}}{{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a\curlyvee{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,b\hskip 3.23753pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.23753pt{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,(a\curlyvee b)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{left={{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$\curlyvee$-introL}\hskip 4.5pt}}

\displaystyle\frac{\textnormal{\small{\raisebox{-1.93748pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}${\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}$-over-$\curlywedge$}}}}}{{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,(a\curlywedge b)\hskip 3.50006pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.50006pt{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,a~{}\curlywedge{}~{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,b}\hskip 20.00003pt\frac{\textnormal{\small{\raisebox{-1.93748pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}${{\raisebox{-0.80997pt}{$\square$}}}$-over-$\curlyvee$}}}}}{{{\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\square$}}}\,a~{}\curlyvee{}~{{\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\square$}}}\,b\hskip 3.50006pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.50006pt{{\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\square$}}}\,(a\curlyvee b)}

We only show a derivation for
\square
-over-
\curlyvee
; the one for
\Diamond
-over-
\curlywedge
is analogous.

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{\textnormal{\footnotesize{\raisebox{-1.72221pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}reflex}}}}}{a\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503pta}}{a\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503pta\curlyvee b}\hskip 8.04996pt\textsc{Left={{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$\curlyvee$-introR}\hskip 3.15pt}}}{{{\raisebox{-0.69296pt}{$\square$}}}\,a\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752pt{{\raisebox{-0.69296pt}{$\square$}}}\,(a\curlyvee b)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Left={{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}${{\raisebox{-0.80997pt}{$\square$}}}$-mono}\hskip 4.5pt}}\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{\textnormal{\footnotesize{\raisebox{-1.72221pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}reflex}}}}}{b\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptb}}{b\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptb\curlyvee a}\enskip\textsc{Right={\hskip 2.25pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$\curlyvee$-introR}}}}{b\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503pt\hbox{\pagecolor{DodgerBlue3!30!white}$a\curlyvee b$}}\hskip 8.04996pt\textsc{Right={\hskip 3.15pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$\curlyvee$-comm}}}}{{{\raisebox{-0.69296pt}{$\square$}}}\,b\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752pt{{\raisebox{-0.69296pt}{$\square$}}}\,(a\curlyvee b)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}${{\raisebox{-0.80997pt}{$\square$}}}$-mono}}}}{{{\raisebox{-0.83246pt}{$\square$}}}\,a~{}\curlyvee{}~{{\raisebox{-0.83246pt}{$\square$}}}\,b\hskip 3.23753pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.23753pt{{\raisebox{-0.83246pt}{$\square$}}}\,(a\curlyvee b)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{left={{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$\curlyvee$-introL}\hskip 4.5pt}}

Note that
\Diamond
indeed only
sub
distributes over
\curlywedge
and so does
\square
over
\curlyvee
.
The converse inequalities do
not
hold in general.

Appendix F
Appendix: Case Studies

F.1
Olszewski Fixed Point Theorems (
\Cref
sec:case_studies:ol)

See
18

Proof.

Consider the following computation:

\displaystyle F\left(\inf_{n\in\omega}~\sup_{k\geq n}~F^{k}(\ell)\right)

\displaystyle~{}={}~\inf_{n\in\omega}~F\left(\sup_{k\geq n}~F^{k}(\ell)\right)

(by assuming
\omega
-cocontinuity of
F
)

\displaystyle~{}\sqsupseteq{}~\inf_{n\in\omega}~\sup_{k\geq n}~F\left(F^{k}(\ell)\right)

(by any monotonic
F
being semi-continuous)

\displaystyle~{}={}~\inf_{n\in\omega}~\sup_{k\geq n}~F^{k+1}(\ell)

\displaystyle~{}={}~\inf_{n}\sup_{k\geq n}F^{k}(\ell)

For the last step, observe that the sequences
(\sup_{k\geq n}F^{k}(\ell))_{n\in\omega}
and
(\sup_{k\geq n}F^{k+1})_{n\in\omega}
are both non-increasing, and the latter is a subsequence the former, so they have the same infimum.
∎

F.2
\boldsymbol{k}
-Induction (
\Cref
sec:case_studies:kinduction)

First recall that in the definition of the
k
-induction operator

G^{k}_{b}\,b~{}={}~\begin{cases}b&\text{if $k=0$}\\
F\,G^{k-1}_{b}\,b~~\curlywedge~~b&\text{otherwise}~.\end{cases}

we mean to define
s
y
ntactic
equality of terms, so e.g. defining that
G^{2}_{b}\,b
is a name for the term
F\,G^{1}_{b}\,b\curlywedge b
.
For our proof, we require a few auxiliary lemmas.

\displaystyle\frac{\textnormal{\small{\raisebox{-1.93748pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$G^{k}_{b}$-deflate}}}}}{G^{k+1}_{b}\,b\hskip 3.50006pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.50006ptG^{k}_{b}\,b}

First, we need that applying
G^{k}_{b}\,
once more to
b
takes us down in the partial order (
G^{k}_{b}
-deflate
), which we prove by induction on
k
.
For
k=0
, we have:

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{\textnormal{\footnotesize{\raisebox{-1.72221pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}reflex}}}}}{b\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptb}}{F\,{G^{0}_{b}\,b}~{}\curlywedge{}~b\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptb}\hskip 8.04996pt\textsc{Left={{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$\curlywedge$-introL}\hskip 3.15pt}}\hskip 8.04996ptG^{0}_{b}\,b\hskip 2.81752pt{}={}\hskip 2.81752ptb}{F\,{G^{0}_{b}\,b}~{}\curlywedge{}~b\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752pt\hbox{\pagecolor{DodgerBlue3!30!white}$G^{0}_{b}\,b$}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Left={{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}indiscern}\hskip 4.5pt}}\hskip 9.24994ptG^{1}_{b}\,b\hskip 3.23753pt{}={}\hskip 3.23753ptF\,G^{0}_{b}\,b~{}\curlywedge{}~b}{\hbox{\pagecolor{DodgerBlue3!30!white}$G^{1}_{b}\,b$}\hskip 3.23753pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.23753ptG^{0}_{b}\,b}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{left={{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}indiscern}\hskip 4.5pt}}

For the induction step, assume validity of
G^{k}_{b}
-deflate
for some arbitrary, but fixed,
k\geq 1
and consider the following:
Below, we omit for conciseness of the proof tree the equalities of the form
G^{k+1}_{b}\,=F\,G^{k}_{b}\,\curlywedge b
on every
indiscern
rule.

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{{\frac{\textnormal{\small\raisebox{-1.93748pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.435,0.655,0.875}\footnotesize{i.h.}}}}}{G^{k}_{b}\,b\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptG^{k-1}_{b}\,b}}}{F\,G^{k}_{b}\,b\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptF\,G^{k-1}_{b}\,b}\enskip\textsc{Left={{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$F$-mono}\hskip 2.25pt}}}{b~{}\curlywedge{}~F\,G^{k}_{b}\,b\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptF\,G^{k-1}_{b}\,b}\enskip\textsc{Left={{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$\curlywedge$-introL}\hskip 2.25pt}}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{DodgerBlue3!30!white}$F\,G^{k}_{b}\,b~{}\curlywedge{}~b$}\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptF\,G^{k-1}_{b}\,b}\enskip\textsc{Left={{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$\curlywedge$-comm}\hskip 2.25pt}}\enskip}{\hbox{\pagecolor{DodgerBlue3!30!white}$G^{k+1}_{b}\,b$}\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptF\,G^{k-1}_{b}\,b}\hskip 8.04996pt\textsc{Left={{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}indiscern}\hskip 3.15pt}}\hskip 8.04996pt\frac{\frac{\frac{\textnormal{\footnotesize{\raisebox{-1.72221pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}reflex}}}}}{b\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptb}}{F\,G^{k}_{b}\,b\curlywedge b\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptb}\enskip\textsc{Right={\hskip 2.25pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$\curlywedge$-introL}}}\enskip}{\hbox{\pagecolor{DodgerBlue3!30!white}$G^{k+1}_{b}\,b$}\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptb}\hskip 8.04996pt\textsc{Right={\hskip 3.15pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}indiscern}}}}{G^{k+1}_{b}\,b\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752ptF\,G^{k-1}_{b}\,b~{}\curlywedge{}~b}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Left={{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$\curlywedge$-introR}\hskip 4.5pt}}\hskip 9.24994pt}{G^{k+1}_{b}\,b\hskip 3.23753pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.23753pt\hbox{\pagecolor{DodgerBlue3!30!white}$G^{k}_{b}\,b$}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}indiscern}}}

\displaystyle\frac{\textnormal{\small{\raisebox{-1.93748pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$G^{k}_{b}$-below}}}}}{G^{k}_{b}\,b\hskip 3.50006pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.50006ptb}

Validity of
G^{k}_{b}
-deflate
(for every
k
) implies that
G^{k}_{b}\,b\preceq b
.
Again, by induction on
k
: For
k=0
, the axiom is trivial.
For the induction step, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{\textnormal{\footnotesize{\raisebox{-1.72221pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$G^{k}_{b}$-deflate}}}}}{G^{k+1}_{b}\,b\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752ptG^{k}_{b}\,b}\hskip 9.24994pt{\frac{\textnormal{\small\raisebox{-1.93748pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.435,0.655,0.875}\footnotesize{i.h.}}}}}{G^{k}_{b}\,b\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752ptb}}}{G^{k}_{b}\,b\hskip 3.23753pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.23753ptb}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}trans}}}

\displaystyle\frac{F\,G^{k}_{b}\,b\hskip 3.50006pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.50006ptb}{F\,G^{k}_{b}\,b\hskip 3.50006pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.50006ptG^{k}_{b}\,b}\hskip 10.00002pt\textsc{right={\hskip 5.0pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$k$-ind-park}}}

As third auxiliary lemma, we need that if
F\,G^{k}_{b}\,b

is below
b
, then
F\,G^{k}_{b}\,b
is even below
G^{k}_{b}\,b
(
k
-ind-park
).
We prove this as by induction on
k
.
The base case
k=0
is trivial.
For the induction step with
k\geq 1
, we have:

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{\textnormal{\footnotesize{\raisebox{-1.72221pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$G^{k}_{b}$-deflate}}}}}{G^{k}_{b}\,b\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptG^{k-1}_{b}\,b}}{F\,G^{k}_{b}\,b\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptF\,G^{k-1}_{b}\,b}\hskip 8.04996pt\textsc{Left={{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$F$-mono}\hskip 3.15pt}}\hskip 8.04996pt{\frac{\textnormal{\small\raisebox{-1.93748pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.435,0.655,0.875}\footnotesize{asm.}}}}}{F\,G^{k}_{b}\,b\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptb}}}{F\,G^{k}_{b}\,b\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752ptF\,G^{k-1}_{b}\,b\curlywedge b}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$\curlywedge$-introR}}}\hskip 9.24994pt}{F\,G^{k}_{b}\,b\hskip 3.23753pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.23753pt\hbox{\pagecolor{DodgerBlue3!30!white}$G^{k}_{b}\,b$}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}indiscern}}}

\displaystyle\frac{{\medcirc}\,b\hskip 3.50006pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.50006ptb}{{\medcirc}\,G^{k}_{b}\,b\hskip 3.50006pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.50006ptG^{k}_{b}\,b}\hskip 10.00002pt\textsc{right={\hskip 5.0pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$G^{k}_{b}\,$-asc-pres}}}

Finally, we require the axiom
G_{b}^{k}
-asc-pres
, which we also prove by induction on
k
.
The base case
k=0
is trivial.
For the induction step, we have:

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{{\frac{\textnormal{\small\raisebox{-1.93748pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.435,0.655,0.875}\footnotesize{i.h.}}}}}{{\medcirc}\,G^{k}_{b}\,b\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptG^{k}_{b}\,b}}}{F\,{\medcirc}\,G^{k}_{b}\,b\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptF\,G^{k}_{b}\,b}\enskip\textsc{Right={\hskip 2.25pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$F$-mono}}}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{DodgerBlue3!30!white}${\medcirc}\,F\,G^{k}_{b}\,b$}\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptF\,G^{k}_{b}\,b}\enskip\textsc{Left={{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$F{\medcirc}\,$-comm}\hskip 2.25pt}}}{{\medcirc}\,F\,G^{k}_{b}\,b\curlywedge{\medcirc}\,b\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptF\,G^{k}_{b}\,b}\enskip\textsc{Left={{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$\curlywedge$-introL}\hskip 2.25pt}}\enskip\frac{{\frac{\textnormal{\small\raisebox{-1.93748pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.435,0.655,0.875}\footnotesize{asm.}}}}}{{\medcirc}\,b\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptb}}}{{\medcirc}\,F\,G^{k}_{b}\,b\curlywedge{\medcirc}\,b\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptb}\enskip\textsc{Right={\hskip 2.25pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$\curlywedge$-introL}}}}{{\medcirc}\,F\,G^{k}_{b}\,b\curlywedge{\medcirc}\,b\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptF\,G^{k}_{b}\,b\curlywedge b}\hskip 8.04996pt\textsc{Left={{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$\curlywedge$-introR}\hskip 3.15pt}}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{DodgerBlue3!30!white}${\medcirc}\,(F\,G^{k}_{b}\,b\curlywedge b)$}\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752ptF\,G^{k}_{b}\,b\curlywedge b}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Left={{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}${\medcirc}$-over-$\curlywedge$}\hskip 4.5pt}}\hskip 9.24994ptG^{k+1}_{b}\,b\hskip 3.23753pt{}={}\hskip 3.23753ptF\,G^{k}_{b}\,b\curlywedge b}{{\medcirc}\,\hbox{\pagecolor{DodgerBlue3!30!white}$G^{k+1}_{b}\,b$}\hskip 3.23753pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.23753pt\hbox{\pagecolor{DodgerBlue3!30!white}$G^{k+1}_{b}\,b$}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}indiscern}}}

Now,
k
-ind
for
k=0
is an immediate instance of
\Cref
thm:generalized_park. For
k\geq 1
, we employ the preceding lemmas, resulting in the following proof:

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\frac{\frac{\frac{\textnormal{\footnotesize{\raisebox{-1.72221pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}bot}}}}}{\bot\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptG^{k}_{b}\,b}\hskip 8.04996pt\frac{{\frac{\textnormal{\small\raisebox{-1.93748pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.435,0.655,0.875}\footnotesize{asm.}}}}}{F\,G^{k}_{b}\,b\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptb}}}{F\,G^{k}_{b}\,b\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptG^{k}_{b}\,b}\hskip 8.04996pt\textsc{Right={\hskip 3.15pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$k$-ind-park}}}\hskip 8.04996pt\hskip 8.04996pt\hskip 8.04996pt\hskip 8.04996pt\hskip 8.04996pt\frac{{\frac{\textnormal{\small\raisebox{-1.93748pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.435,0.655,0.875}\footnotesize{asm.}}}}}{{\medcirc}\,b\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptb}}}{{\medcirc}\,G^{k}_{b}\,b\hskip 1.57503pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 1.57503ptG^{k}_{b}\,b\vphantom{\Bigl(}}\hskip 8.04996pt\textsc{Right={\hskip 3.15pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$G^{k}_{b}\,$-asc-pres}}}}{{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}\bot\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752ptG^{k}_{b}\,b}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Left={{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}tkp-least}\hskip 4.5pt}}\hskip 9.24994pt\frac{\textnormal{\footnotesize{\raisebox{-1.72221pt}{{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}$G^{k}_{b}$-below}}}}}{G^{k}_{b}\,b\hskip 2.81752pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 2.81752ptb}}{{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\Diamond$}}\,F^{*}\bot\hskip 3.23753pt{}\preceq{}\hskip 3.23753ptb}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{Right={\hskip 4.5pt{}{\color[rgb]{0.5625,0.5625,0.5625}trans}}}

∎

Appendix G
Appendix: Incompleteness of Finitary Axiom Systems (
\Cref
sec:incompleteness)

We give a detailed account of Theorem
23
, which states that the finitary quasiequational theory of the operators
{\circleddash}
and
{\medcirc}
on sequences has no finite axiomatization.

G.1
Finitary Quasiequations in General

We first recall some standard terminology and notation from universal algebra, generalizing the concepts introduced in
\Cref
sec:synsem from sequence algebras to algebras over any finitary signature.

Notation 31
.

Fix a finitary algebraic signature
\Sigma
, i.e. a set of operation symbols with associated finite arities.
Moreover, fix a countably infinite set

\mathsf{Vars}~{}={}~\{x,y,z,\ldots\}

of variables, and let
\Sigma^{*}\mathsf{Vars}
denote the set of
\Sigma
-terms formed over the set
\mathsf{Vars}
of variables. A
\Sigma
-algebra is a set
A
equipped with an operation
\mathsf{f}^{A}\colon A^{n}\to A
for every
n
-ary operation symbol in
\Sigma
. Given a
\Sigma
-algebra
A
, a variable interpretation
\mathfrak{I}\colon\mathsf{Vars}\to A
and a term
t\in\Sigma^{*}\mathsf{Vars}
, we write
{{}^{\mathfrak{I}}\llbracket t\rrbracket}\in A
for the value of the term
t
in the algebra
A
under the given interpretation. Formally,
{{}^{\mathfrak{I}}\llbracket t\rrbracket}
is defined inductively by

{{}^{\mathfrak{I}}\llbracket x\rrbracket}~{}={}~\mathfrak{I}(x)\hskip 18.49988pt\textnormal{and}\hskip 18.49988pt{{}^{\mathfrak{I}}\llbracket\mathsf{f}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\rrbracket}~{}={}~\mathsf{f}^{A}({{}^{\mathfrak{I}}\llbracket t_{1}\rrbracket},\ldots,{{}^{\mathfrak{I}}\llbracket t_{n}\rrbracket})~,

where
x\in\mathcal{V}
,
\mathsf{f}
is an
n
-ary operation symbol from
\Sigma
, and
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}\in\Sigma^{*}\mathcal{V}
. Finally, given a substitution (a map
\sigma\colon\mathsf{Vars}\to\Sigma^{*}\mathsf{Vars}
) and a term
t\in\Sigma^{*}\mathsf{Vars}
, we write
t[\sigma]\in\Sigma^{*}\mathsf{Vars}
for the term obtained by applying the substitution
\sigma
to the variables of
t
.

\lipicsEnd

Definition 32
.

1.

A
(
\Sigma
-)identity
is an expression of the form

s~{}={}~t~,\hskip 18.49988pt\text{where $s,t\in\Sigma^{*}\mathsf{Vars}$}.

A
(finitary
\Sigma
-)quasiequation
is an expression of the form

P\hskip 9.24994pt{}\Longrightarrow{}\hskip 9.24994pts~{}={}~t~,

(7)

where
s=t
is an identity and
P
is a finite set of identities. The quasiequation (
7
) is
satisfied
in a
\Sigma
-algebra
A
if for every variable interpretation
\mathfrak{I}\colon\mathsf{Vars}\to A
,

{{}^{\mathfrak{I}}\llbracket u\rrbracket}~{}={}~{{}^{\mathfrak{I}}\llbracket v\rrbracket}\text{ , for all $u=v$ in $P$,}\hskip 9.24994pt\textnormal{implies}\hskip 9.24994pt{{}^{\mathfrak{I}}\llbracket s\rrbracket}~{}={}~{{}^{\mathfrak{I}}\llbracket t\rrbracket}.

(8)

2.

The
substitution instance
of (
7
) for a substitution
\sigma
is the finitary quasiequation

P[\sigma]\hskip 9.24994pt{}\Longrightarrow{}\hskip 9.24994pts[\sigma]~{}={}~t[\sigma]~,\hskip 18.49988pt\text{ where $P[\sigma]~{}={}~\{u[\sigma]=v[\sigma]\mid(u=v)\in P\}$}.

Notation 32
.

We commonly write

s_{1}=t_{1}\,\wedge\,\,\ldots,\,\wedge\,s_{n}=t_{n}\hskip 9.24994pt{}\Longrightarrow{}\hskip 9.24994pts~{}={}~t

or simply

s_{1}=t_{1},\,\ldots,\,s_{n}=t_{n}\hskip 9.24994pt{}\Longrightarrow{}\hskip 9.24994pts~{}={}~t

for a quasiequation

\{s_{1}=t_{1},\,\ldots,\,s_{n}=t_{n}\}\hskip 9.24994pt{}\Longrightarrow{}\hskip 9.24994pts~{}={}~t

This notation allows to freely permute or duplicate premises.

\lipicsEnd

Example 33
.

We denote by
\mathsf{Ax}_{=}
the following set of quasiequations, where
\mathsf{f}
ranges over operations in
\Sigma
and
n
is the arity of
\mathsf{f}
.

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt{}\Longrightarrow{}\hskip 9.24994ptx=x

(9)

\displaystyle x=y

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt{}\Longrightarrow{}\hskip 9.24994pty=z

(10)

\displaystyle x=y,\,y=z

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt{}\Longrightarrow{}\hskip 9.24994ptx=z

(11)

\displaystyle x_{1}=y_{1},\,\ldots\,x_{n}=y_{n}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt{}\Longrightarrow{}\hskip 9.24994pt\mathsf{f}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=\mathsf{f}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})

(12)

These correspond to axioms of standard equational logic and thus hold in every
\Sigma
-algebra.

\lipicsEnd

Remark 34
.

If an algebra
A
satisfies a quasiequation
q
, then
A
satisfies all substitution instances of
q
as well.

\lipicsEnd

Definition 35
.

Given a set
\mathsf{Ax}
of finitary quasiequations, a
proof
of a finitary quasiequation

P\Longrightarrow s=t

from
\mathsf{Ax}
is a finite tree with the following properties:

1.

Every node is labelled with some identity
u=v
where
u,v\in\Sigma^{*}\mathsf{Vars}
.

2.

The root is labelled with
s=t
.

3.

For every inner node labelled
u=v
with children labelled
u_{1}=v_{1},\ldots,u_{n}=v_{n}
, the quasiequation

u_{1}=v_{1},\ldots,u_{n}=v_{n}\Longrightarrow u=v

is a substitution instance of some quasiequation in
\mathsf{Ax}\cup\mathsf{Ax}_{=}
.

4.

For every leaf node, the label
u=v
is either an identity in
P
or a substitution instance of some premise-free quasiequation in
\mathsf{Ax}\cup\mathsf{Ax}_{=}
.

A quasiequation
P\Longrightarrow s=t
is
\mathsf{Ax}
-provable
, denoted

\mathsf{Ax}\vdash P\Longrightarrow s=t,

if there exists a proof of it from
\mathsf{Ax}
. It is
provable in equational logic
if it is
\emptyset
-provable, denoted

\vdash P\Longrightarrow s=t.

Remark 36
.

If
\mathsf{Ax}\vdash P\Longrightarrow s=t
, then every
\mathsf{Ax}\cup\{P\Longrightarrow s=t\}
-provable quasiequation is
\mathsf{Ax}
-provable. In other words, one can treat quasiequations provable from
\mathsf{Ax}
like additional axioms. Indeed, any use of a substitution instance
P[\sigma]\Longrightarrow s[\sigma]=t[\sigma]
in a proof from
\mathsf{Ax}\cup\{P\Longrightarrow s=t\}
can be replaced with a proof of
P[\sigma]\Longrightarrow s[\sigma]=t[\sigma]
from
\mathsf{Ax}
.

\lipicsEnd

G.2
Discrete Sequence Algebras

Definition 37

(Discrete Sequence Algebras)
.

A
discrete sequence algebra
is given by the set
A^{\omega}
of sequences over a set
A
equipped with the unary operations

{\circleddash}\,\colon A^{\omega}\to A^{\omega},\hskip 18.49988pt{\circleddash}\,(\varphi)=(\varphi(0),\varphi(0),\varphi(0),\ldots),

and

{\medcirc}\,\colon A^{\omega}\to A^{\omega},\hskip 18.49988pt{\medcirc}\,(\varphi)=(\varphi(1),\varphi(2),\varphi(3),\ldots).

Note that discrete sequence algebras are algebras for the algebraic signature
\Delta
given by the unary operation symbols
{\circleddash}
and
{\medcirc}
:

\Delta=\{{\circleddash}\,:1,\,{\medcirc}\,:1\}.

Definition 38
.

1.

A
{\medcirc}
-term

term is a term of the form
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)
, where
k\geq 0
and
x\in\mathsf{Vars}
.

2.

A
{\medcirc}
-identity
is an identity
s=t
between
{\medcirc}
-terms.

3.

A
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,
-term

term is a term of the form
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)
, where
k\geq 0
and
x\in\mathsf{Vars}
.

4.

A
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,
-identity
is an identity
s=t
between
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,
-terms.

5.

A
homogeneous identity
is either a
{\medcirc}
-identity or a
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,
-identity.

Definition 39
.

A finitary
\Delta
-quasiequation is
valid for discrete sequence algebras
if it satisfied in every discrete sequence algebra.

In the remainder of
\Cref
app:incompleteness, by a
quasiequation
we always mean a finitary
\Delta
-quasiequation, and by a
valid quasiequation
we mean one that is valid for discrete sequence algebras.

Example 40
.

The following quasiequations are valid:

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow{\circleddash}\,(x)={\circleddash}\,^{2}(x)

(13)

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,{\circleddash}\,(x)={\circleddash}\,(x)

(14)

\displaystyle x={\circleddash}\,(y)

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow{\circleddash}\,(x)={\circleddash}\,(y)

(15)

\displaystyle x={\circleddash}\,(y)

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,(x)=x

(16)

\lipicsEnd

Remark 41
.

If
\mathsf{Ax}
is a set of valid quasiequations, then every
\mathsf{Ax}
-provable quasiequation is valid. This is immediate from
\Cref
rem:subst by induction on the height of proof trees.

\lipicsEnd

We start with a few technical results on quasiequations provable in equational logic.

Lemma 42
.

If
\vdash P\Longrightarrow s=t
and all identities in
P
are homogeneous, then

1.

s
is a
{\medcirc}
-term iff
t
is a
{\medcirc}
-term.

2.

s
is a
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,
-term iff
t
is a
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,
-term.

Proof.

Immediate by induction on the height of a proof tree for
P\Longrightarrow s=t
.
∎

Proofs of quasiequations in equational logic have a convenient graphical presentation:

Definition 43
.

To every set
P
of homogeneous identities we associate the infinite undirected graph
G(P)
whose nodes are all pairs
(x,n)
where
x\in\mathsf{Vars}
and
n
is a non-negative integer, and with two types of edges for
(x,n)\neq(y,k)
:

•

If
\vdash P\Longrightarrow{{\medcirc}\,}^{n}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{k}(y)
, then there is a
strong
edge between
(x,n)
and
(y,k)
.

•

If
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{n}(x)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(y)
lies in
P
and the quasiequation
P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{n}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{k}(y)
is not provable in equational logic, then there is a
weak
edge between
(x,n)
and
(y,k)
.

By a
strong path
in
G(P)
we mean a path consisting of strong edges.

Proposition 44
.

Let
P
be a set of homogeneous identities,
n,k\geq 0
and
x,y\in\mathsf{Vars}
.

1.

\vdash P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{n}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{k}(y)
iff there exists a strong path from
(x,n)
to
(y,k)
in
G(P)
.

2.

\vdash P\Longrightarrow{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{n}(x)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(y)
iff there exists a path from
(x,n)
to
(y,k)
in
G(P)
.

Proof.

1.

(
\Longrightarrow
) Suppose that
\vdash P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{n}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{k}(y)
. If
(x,n)=(y,k)
, there is a trivial path of length
0
from
(x,n)
to
(y,k)
. If
(x,n)\neq(y,k)
, there is a strong edge from
(x,n)
to
(y,k)
, that is, an strong path of length
1
.

(
\Longleftarrow
) Suppose that there exists a strong path from
(x,n)
to
(y,k)
in
G(P)
, say

(x,n)=(x_{0},n_{0}),\,(x_{1},n_{1})\,\ldots,\,(x_{l},n_{l})=(y,k).

This means that

\vdash P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{n_{i}}(x_{i})={\medcirc}\,^{n_{i+1}}(x_{i+1})\hskip 18.49988pt(0\leq i<l)

and therefore

\vdash P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{n}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{k}(y)

via reflexivity (
9
) and transitivity (
11
).

2.

(
\Longleftarrow
) Suppose that there exists a path from
(x,n)
to
(y,k)
in
G(P)
, say

(x,n)=(x_{0},n_{0}),\,(x_{1},n_{1})\,\ldots,\,(x_{l},n_{l})=(y,k).

Then

\vdash P\Longrightarrow{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{n_{i}}(x_{i})={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{n_{i+1}}(x_{i+1})\hskip 18.49988pt(0\leq i<l).

(17)

Indeed, for each
i
, if there is a strong edge from
(x_{i},n_{i})
to
(x_{i+1},n_{i+1})
, then

\vdash P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{n_{i}}(x_{i})={\medcirc}\,^{n_{i+1}}(x_{i+1})

and therefore

\vdash P\Longrightarrow{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{n_{i}}(x_{i})={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{n_{i+1}}(x_{i+1})

by congruence (
12
). If there is a weak edge from
(x_{i},n_{i})
to
(x_{i+1},n_{i+1})
, then the identity
{\medcirc}\,^{n_{i}}(x_{i})={\medcirc}\,^{n_{i+1}}(x_{i+1})
lies in
P
and therefore trivially

\vdash P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{n_{i}}(x_{i})={\medcirc}\,^{n_{i+1}}(x_{i+1}).

From (
17
) we now get

\vdash P\Longrightarrow{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{n}(x)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(y)

via reflexivity (
9
) and transitivity (
11
).

(
\Longrightarrow
) Suppose that

\vdash P\Longrightarrow{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{n}(x)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(y).

(18)

We prove that there exists a path from
(x,n)
to
(y,k)
in
G(P)
. The proof is by induction of the height of a proof tree for (
18
). For a proof of height
0
, the identity
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{n}(x)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(y)
is either an element of
P
, in which case there is an edge from
(x,n)
to
(y,k)
, or it is substitution instance of reflexivity (
9
), i.e. the terms
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{n}(x)
and
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(y)
are identical and there is a path of length
0
.

Now consider a proof tree of positive height. If the last step is an substitution instance of the symmetry rule (
10
), then there is a proof of

P\Longrightarrow{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(y)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{n}(x)

of smaller height. By induction, there is a path from
(y,k)
to
(x,n)
, hence from
(x,n)
to
(y,k)
. If the last step is an instance of the transitivity rule (
11
), then there are proofs of smaller height of

P\Longrightarrow{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{n}(x)=u\hskip 18.49988pt\textnormal{and}\hskip 18.49988ptP\Longrightarrow u={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(y)

for some term
u
. By
\Cref
lem:hom-proof, the term
u
must have the form
u={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{m}(v)

for some
m\geq 0
and
v\in\mathsf{Vars}
. By induction, there is a path from
(x,n)
to
(v,m)
and from
(v,m)
to
(y,k)
, hence from
(x,n)
to
(y,k)
. If the last step is an instance of the congruence rule (
12
), then there exists a proof of smaller height of

P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{n}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{k}(y).

Thus, there is a (strong) edge from
(x,n)
to
(y,k)
, i.e. a path of length
1
.∎

Lemma 45
.

Let
P
be a finite set of homogeneous identities and
x,y\in\mathsf{Vars}
. For all
k<l
and
m,n\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\vdash{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(x),\,{\medcirc}\,^{m}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{n}(y)

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{m^{\prime}}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{n}(y)

\displaystyle\vdash{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(x),\,{\medcirc}\,^{m^{\prime}}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{n}(y)

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{m}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{n}(y)

where

m^{\prime}=k+((m-k)\mathrel{\mathrm{mod}}(l-k)).

Proof.

By definition of
m^{\prime}
, one has
m=p\cdot(l-k)+m^{\prime}
for some integer
p
. From this the statements are immediate.
∎

Lemma 46
.

Let
P
be a finite set of homogeneous identities and
x\in\mathsf{Vars}
. If no quasiequation
P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(x)
where
k\neq l
is provable in equational logic, then the following holds:

1.

There exist only finitely many pairs
k\neq l
such that
\vdash P\Longrightarrow{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{l}(x)
.

2.

If moreover
y\in\mathsf{Vars}
and
\vdash P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{m}(y)={\medcirc}\,^{n}(y)
for some
m\neq n
, then for every fixed
q
there exist only finitely many
k
with
\vdash P\Longrightarrow{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{q}(y)
.

Proof.

Suppose that no quasiequation
P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(x)
where
k\neq l
is not provable in equational logic.

1.

Consider the graph
G(P)
associated to
P
. For every node
(y,n)
, there exists at most one node
(x,k)
with a strong edge from
(y,n)
to
(x,k)
. Indeed, if there exists a second such node
(x,l)
,
k\neq l
, we have
\vdash P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(x)
, a contradiction. Consider the set
C\subseteq\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
given by

\displaystyle(n,k)\in C

\displaystyle\iff\text{there exists $y\in\mathop{\mathsf{Var}}$ such that}

(i) there ex. a weak edge containing the node
(y,n)
, and

(ii) there ex. a strong edge (equival.: a strong path) from
(y,n)
to
(x,k)
.

Since
P
is finite and by the above argument, the set
C
is finite. Pick an upper bound
K
such that

\forall(n,k)\in C.\,k\leq K.

Now suppose that

\vdash P\Longrightarrow{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{l}(x)

(19)

where
k\neq l
. Then there exists a path from
(x,k)
to
(x,l)
in
G(P)
At least one of its edges is weak; otherwise, by
\Cref
prop:proof-graph, we have
\vdash P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(x)
, a contradiction. Let
(y,n)
be the end node of the last weak edge on the path. Then the subpath from
(y,n)
to
(x,l)
is strong, and so
(n,l)\in C
, which implies
l\leq K
. By considering the reversed path from
(x,l)
to
(x,k)
instead, we see that
k\leq K
. Hence there are only finitely many pairs
k\neq l
with (
19
)

2.

Suppose that
\vdash P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{m}(y)={\medcirc}\,^{n}(y)
for some
m\neq n
. Then no quasiequation
P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{i}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{j}(y)
is provable in equational logic. (Otherwise, this proof could be used to construct a proof of some
P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(x)
where
k\neq l
.) Now fix
q
and let
\vdash P\Longrightarrow{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{q}(y)
. Then there exists a path from
(x,k)
to
(y,q)
in
D(P)
, and by the above argument this path is not strong. Let
(z,p)
be the start node of the first weak edge on the path. Consider the set
C
and the upper bound
K
as above. Then
(p,k)\in C
and so
k\leq K
, which implies that there only finitely many choices of
k
.

∎

Lemma 47
.

Let
P
be a finite set of homogeneous identities and
x,y\in\mathsf{Vars}
. If
x\neq y
and no quasiequation
P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(x)
or
P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{k}(y)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(y)
for
k\neq l
is provable in equational logic, then the following holds:

1.

For all
p,p^{\prime},q,q^{\prime}
, if
\vdash P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{p}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{q}(y)
and
\vdash P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{p^{\prime}}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{q^{\prime}}(y)
then
p-q\,=p^{\prime}-q^{\prime}
.

2.

There exist only finitely many pairs
k,l
such that
\vdash P\Longrightarrow{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{l}(y)
and the quasiequation
P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(y)
is not provable in equational logic.

Proof.

Let
x\neq y
and suppose that no quasiequation
P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(x)
or
P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{k}(y)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(y)
where
k\neq l
is provable in equational logic.

1.

Suppose that
\vdash P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{p}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{q}(y)
and
\vdash P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{p^{\prime}}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{q^{\prime}}(y)
. If
q=q^{\prime}
, it follows by symmetry and transitivity that
\vdash P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{p}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{p^{\prime}}(x)
, and so
p=p^{\prime}
by hypothesis, i.e.
p-q=p^{\prime}-q^{\prime}
. Now suppose that
q\neq q^{\prime}
, say
q<q^{\prime}
. Then
\vdash P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{p+(q^{\prime}-q)}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{q^{\prime}}(x)
by congruence and so
\vdash P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{p+(q^{\prime}-q)}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{p^{\prime}}(x)
by symmetry and transitivity. This implies
p+(q^{\prime}-q)=p^{\prime}
by hypothesis, i.e.
p-q=p^{\prime}-q^{\prime}
.

2.

Suppose that

\vdash P\Longrightarrow{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{l}(y)

(20)

and the quasiequation
P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(y)
is not provable in equational logic. Consider again the set
C
and the upper bound
K
as defined in the proof of
\Cref
lem:eq-logic-2. By hypothesis, there is a path from
(x,k)
to
(y,l)
in
G(P)
but the path is not strong. Let
(z,n)
be the start node of the first weak edge on the path. Then
(n,k)\in C
and so
k\leq K
. A symmetric argument, swapping the roles of
x
and
y
, shows that also
l
is bounded from above by some constant
L
. This shows that there are only finitely many pairs
k,l
satisfying (
20
).∎

Proposition 48
.

Let
P
be a finite set of homogeneous identities, and let
x
and
y
be distinct variables. Then there exists a finite set
B
of homogeneous identities over the variables
x
and
y
such that

(u=v)\in B\hskip 9.24994pt\text{implies}\hskip 9.24994pt\vdash P\Longrightarrow u=v,

(21)

and moreover, for any homogeneous identity
u=v
where
u,v\in\Delta^{*}\{x,y\}
,

\vdash P\Longrightarrow u=v\hskip 18.49988pt\text{iff}\hskip 18.49988pt\vdash B\Longrightarrow u=v.

(22)

We call such a set
B
a
basis
for
P
w.r.t.
x
and
y
.

Proof.

Let
D(P)
be the set of all homogeneous identities
u=v
where
u,v\in\Delta^{*}\{x,y\}
and

\vdash P\Longrightarrow u=v.

For every finite subset
B\subseteq D(P)
, the implication (
21
) and the right-to-left implication of (
22
) clearly hold. We show that there is a choice of
B
for which also the left-to-right implication of (
22
) holds. For this purpose, we need to distinguish several cases:

Case 1:

D(P)
contains two identities
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(x)
and
{\medcirc}\,^{m}(y)={\medcirc}\,^{n}(y)
where
k\neq l
and
m\neq n
.

Choose two such pairs
k,l
and
m,n
, where w.l.o.g. we assume
k<l
and
m<n
. Let
B\subseteq D(P)
be given by

{romanenumerate}

all identities
{\medcirc}\,^{p}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{q}(x)
and
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{p}(x)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{q}(x)
in
D(P)
where
k\leq p,q\leq l
;

all identities
{\medcirc}\,^{p}(y)={\medcirc}\,^{q}(y)
and
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{p}(y)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{q}(y)
in
D(P)
where
m\leq p,q\leq n
;

all identities
{\medcirc}\,^{p}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{q}(y)
and
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{p}(x)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{q}(y)
in
D(P)
where
k\leq p\leq l
and
m\leq q\leq n
;

Clearly,
B
is a finite set. We claim that the left-to-right implication of (
22
) holds. Thus suppose that
\vdash P\Longrightarrow u=v
. We only consider the case that
u=v
is of the form
{\medcirc}\,^{p}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{q}(y)
, since the other cases are entirely analogous. By
\Cref
lem:eq-logic-1 there exists an identity
{\medcirc}\,^{p^{\prime}}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{q^{\prime}}(y)
in
B
(namely, take
p^{\prime}=k+((p-k)\mathrel{\mathrm{mod}}(l-k))
and
q^{\prime}=m+((q-m)\mathrel{\mathrm{mod}}(n-m))
) such that

\vdash{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(x),\,{\medcirc}\,^{m}(y)={\medcirc}\,^{n}(y),\,{\medcirc}\,^{p^{\prime}}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{q^{\prime}}(y)\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{p}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{q}(y).

Since all three identities in the premise lie in
B
, we conclude
\vdash B\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{p}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{q}(y)
.

Case 2:

D(P)
contains no identity
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(x)
where
k\neq l
but contains some identity
{\medcirc}\,^{m}(y)={\medcirc}\,^{n}(y)
where
m\neq n
.

Choose such a pair
m,n
, and assume w.l.o.g. that
m<n
. Let
B\subseteq D(P)
be given by

{romanenumerate}

all identities
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{l}(x)
,
k\neq l
, in
D(P)
.

all identities
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{q}(y)
in
D(P)
where
m\leq q\leq n
;

all identities
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{p}(y)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{q}(y)
and
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{p}(x)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{q}(x)
in
D(P)
where
m\leq p,q\leq n
;

Then
B
is a finite set by
\Cref
lem:eq-logic-2. Moreover,
\vdash P\Longrightarrow u=v
implies
\vdash B\Longrightarrow u=v
:

•

For
u=v
of the form
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(x)
, one has
k=l
by hypothesis, so
\vdash B\Longrightarrow u=v
by reflexivity.

•

For
u=v
of the form
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{l}(x)
, if
k=l
then
\vdash B\Longrightarrow u=v
by reflexivity, and if
k\neq l
, then the identity
u=v
is contained in
B
, so
\vdash B\Longrightarrow u=v
trivially.

•

u=v
cannot be of the form
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{q}(y)
, for otherwise from this and
\vdash P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{m}(y)={\medcirc}\,^{n}(y)
a non-trivial
P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{p}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{q}(x)
is provable.

•

For
u=v
of the form
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{q}(y)
, one can assume w.l.o.g. that
m\leq q\leq n
by
\Cref
lem:eq-logic-2, and then
u=v
lies in
B
, so
\vdash B\Longrightarrow u=v
.

•

For
u=v
of the form
{\medcirc}\,^{p}(y)={\medcirc}\,^{q}(y)
or
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{p}(y)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{q}(y)
, one can assume w.l.o.g. that
m\leq p,q\leq n
by
\Cref
lem:eq-logic-1, and then
u=v
lies in
B
, so
\vdash B\Longrightarrow u=v
.

Case 3:

D(P)
contains some identity
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(x)
where
k\neq l
but no identity
{\medcirc}\,^{m}(y)={\medcirc}\,^{n}(y)
where
m\neq n
.

Symmetric to Case 2.

Case 4.

D(P)
contains no identity
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(x)
where
k\neq l
and no identity
{\medcirc}\,^{m}(y)={\medcirc}\,^{n}(y)
where
m\neq n
.

We call an identity
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{m}(y)
in
D(P)

minimal
if there exists no identity
{\medcirc}\,^{k^{\prime}}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{m^{\prime}}(y)
with
k^{\prime}<k
. By
\Cref
lem:eq-logic-2, there exists at most one minimal identity in
D(P)
.

We let
B\subseteq D(P)
be given by

{romanenumerate}

all identities
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{l}(x)
,
k\neq l
, in
D(P)
;

the minimal identity in
D(P)
, if any;

all identities
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{m}(y)
in
D(P)
such that
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{m}(y)
is not contained in
D(P)
.

all identities
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{m}(y)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{m}(y)
,
m\neq n
, in
D(P)
.

Then
B
is a finite set by
\Cref
lem:eq-logic-2 and
\Cref
lem:eq-logic-3. Moreover,
\vdash P\Longrightarrow u=v
implies
\vdash B\Longrightarrow u=v
:

•

For
u=v
of the form
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(x)
or
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(y)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(y)
, one has
k=l
and so
\vdash B\Longrightarrow u=v
by reflexivity.

•

For
u=v
of the form
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{l}(x)
or
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(y)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{l}(y)
, the identity
u=v
lies in
B
, so
\vdash B\Longrightarrow u=v
trivially.

•

For
u=v
of the form
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{m}(y)
, the identity is provable via congruence from the minimal identity in
D(P)
by
\Cref
lem:eq-logic-3, and so
\vdash B\Longrightarrow u=v
since the latter lies in
B
.

•

For
u=v
of the form
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{m}(y)
, if
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{m}(y)
in
D(P)
, then
u=v
is provable from the minimal identity (which lies in
B
) via congruence; otherwise, the identity
u=v
lies in
B
. In both cases,
\vdash B\Longrightarrow u=v
.

This concludes the proof of (
22
).
∎

Proposition 49
.

Let
P
a finite set of homogeneous identities, and let
B
be a finite basis for
P
w.r.t.
x
and
y
.
Then for any homogeneous identity
u=v
where
u,v\in\Delta^{*}\{x,y\}
,

P\Longrightarrow u=v\text{ is valid}\hskip 18.49988pt\text{iff}\hskip 18.49988ptB\Longrightarrow u=v\text{ is valid}.

(23)

Proof.

We use the notation
D(P)
from the proof of
\Cref
prop:basis.
Clearly, since
B\subseteq D(P)
, the right-to-left implication holds. Conversely, suppose that
P\Longrightarrow u=v
is valid, let
A
be a set, and let
\mathfrak{I}\colon\mathsf{Vars}\to A^{\omega}
be a variable interpretation such that

{{}^{\mathfrak{I}}\llbracket s\rrbracket}={{}^{\mathfrak{I}}\llbracket t\rrbracket}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all $s=t$ in B}.

(24)

It suffices show that there exists an interpretation
{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}\colon\mathsf{Vars}\to A^{\omega}
such that

{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}(x)=\mathfrak{I}(x),\hskip 9.24994pt{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}(y)=\mathfrak{I}(y),\hskip 9.24994pt{{}^{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}\llbracket s\rrbracket}={{}^{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}\llbracket t\rrbracket}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{for all $s=t$ in P}.

(25)

Then, since
P\Longrightarrow u=v
is valid and
u,v\in\Delta^{*}\{x,y\}
, it follows that

{{}^{\mathfrak{I}}\llbracket u\rrbracket}={{}^{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}\llbracket u\rrbracket}={{}^{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}\llbracket v\rrbracket}={{}^{\mathfrak{I}}\llbracket v\rrbracket}

proving that
B\Longrightarrow u=v
is valid. For the definition of
{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}
, we consider the graph
G(P)
(
\Cref
def:graph). For each connected component
C\subseteq\mathsf{Vars}\times\mathbb{N}
, we define an element
a_{C}\in A
as follows:

•

If
C
contains a node
(z,n)\in C
where
z\in\{x,y\}
, put

a_{C}:=\mathfrak{I}(z)(n).

Note that
a_{C}
is independent of the choice of
(z,n)
: if
(w,m)
is another element of
C
where
w\in\{x,y\}
, then there exists a path from
(z,n)
to
(w,m)
in
G(P)
, so

\vdash P\Longrightarrow{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{n}(z)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{m}(w).

This implies

\vdash B\Longrightarrow{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{n}(z)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{m}(w)

by (
22
), in particular the quasiequation
B\Longrightarrow{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{n}(z)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{m}(w)
is valid. Therefore, from (
24
) it follows that
{{}^{\mathfrak{I}}\llbracket{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{n}(z)\rrbracket}={{}^{\mathfrak{I}}\llbracket{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(w)\rrbracket}
, which means

\mathfrak{I}(z)(n)=\mathfrak{I}(w)(k).

•

If
C
does not contain any node
(z,n)
where
z\in\{x,y\}
, choose an arbitrary element
a_{C}\in A
.

Now define
{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}\colon\mathsf{Vars}\to A^{\omega}
by

{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}(z)(n)=a_{C_{(z,n)}}\hskip 18.49988pt\text{where $C_{(z,n)}$ is the connected component of $(z,n)$ in $G(P)$.}

We show that
{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}
satisfies the desired properties (
25
). Indeed, if
z\in\{x,y\}
, then

{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}(z)(n)=a_{C_{(z,n)}}=\mathfrak{I}(z)(n)\hskip 18.49988pt\text{for all $n\geq 0$}

by definition of
a_{C_{(z,n)}}
, and so
\mathfrak{I}(z)={\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}(z)
. Now suppose that
s=t
is an identity in
P
. If
s=t
is a
{\medcirc}
-identity
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(z)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(w)
then for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
the pairs
(z,k+n)
and
(w,l+n)
lie in the same connected component of
G(P)
since they are connected by a strong edge, and so

\displaystyle{{}^{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}\llbracket s\rrbracket}(n)

\displaystyle={{}^{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}\llbracket{\medcirc}\,^{k}(z)\rrbracket}(n)={\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}(z)(k+n)

\displaystyle=a_{C_{(z,k+n)}}=a_{C_{(w,l+n)}}

\displaystyle={\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}(w)(l+n)={{}^{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}\llbracket{\medcirc}\,^{l}(w)\rrbracket}(n)={{}^{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}\llbracket t\rrbracket}(n)~,

whence
{{}^{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}\llbracket s\rrbracket}={{}^{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}\llbracket t\rrbracket}
. Similarly, if
s=t
is a
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,
-identity
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(z)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{l}(w)
, then the pairs
(z,k)
and
(w,l)
lie in the same connected component of
G(P)
since they are connected by a (strong or weak) edge, whence

{{}^{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}\llbracket s\rrbracket}={{}^{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}\llbracket{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(z)\rrbracket}=({\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}(z)(k))^{\omega}=(a_{C_{(z,k)}})^{\omega}=(a_{C_{(w,l)}})^{\omega}=({\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}(w)(l))^{\omega}={{}^{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}\llbracket{\medcirc}\,^{l}(w)\rrbracket}={{}^{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}\llbracket t\rrbracket}.

This concludes the proof.
∎

The existence of finite bases allows us to reduce quasiequations to a very simple form:

Definition 50
.

Fix a pair of distinct variables
x\neq y
. A quasiequation
P\Longrightarrow s=t
is
normalized (w.r.t.
x
and
y
)
if the following conditions hold:

1.

All identities in
P
are homogeneous.

2.

The term
s
and
t
and all terms occurring in
P
are elements of
\Delta^{*}\{x,y\}
.

3.

P
contains no identity of the form
{\medcirc}\,^{l}(y)={\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)
.

4.

All identities in
P
of the form
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(y)
have the same degree
k
on the left hand side.

5.

If the identities
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(y)
and
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{m}(y)
lie in
P
, then so does
{\medcirc}\,^{l}(y)={\medcirc}\,^{m}(y)
.

Definition 51
.

Given a set
\mathsf{Ax}
of quasiequations, a set
\mathsf{Ax}^{\prime}
of quasiequations
strengthens

\mathsf{Ax}
if every quasiequation in
\mathsf{Ax}
is
\mathsf{Ax}^{\prime}
-provable (equivalently, by
\Cref
rem:proof-props, if every
\mathsf{Ax}
-provable quasiequation is
\mathsf{Ax}^{\prime}
-provable).

Theorem 52

(Normalization)
.

For every finite set
\mathsf{Ax}
of valid quasiequations, there exists a finite set
\mathsf{Ax}^{\prime}
of valid quasiequations such that
\mathsf{Ax}^{\prime}
strengthens
\mathsf{Ax}
and all quasiequations contained in
\mathsf{Ax}^{\prime}
except (
13
)–(
16
) are normalized.

Proof.

We may assume that
\mathsf{Ax}
contains the quasiequations (
13
)–(
16
), which are all valid; otherwise, add them to
\mathsf{Ax}
. We show how to replace, step by step, non-normalized quasiequations in
\mathsf{Ax}
(i.e. quasiequations
not
satisfying one of the requirements
1
–
5
) with equivalent normalized ones, until a set of valid quasiequations is reached that strenghtens
\mathsf{Ax}
and contains, except for (
13
)–(
16
), only normalized quasiequations.

[N1.]

1.

Suppose that
\mathsf{Ax}
contains, except from (
13
)–(
16
), some quasiequation of the form

P,\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(z)=u\Longrightarrow s=t

(26)

where
u
contains
{\circleddash}
. (The case
u={\medcirc}\,^{k}(z)
is handled symmetrically.) Thus
u=v({\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{m}(w))
for some unique term
v
and variable
w
. Replace (
26
) with

P,\,{\medcirc}\,^{k+1}(z)={\medcirc}\,^{k}(z),\,{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(z)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{m}(w)\Longrightarrow s=t.

(27)

This quasiequation is valid since the identity
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(z)=v({\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{m}(w))
is semantically equivalent to the conjunction of
{\medcirc}\,^{k+1}(z)={\medcirc}\,^{k}(z)
and
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(z)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{m}(w)
. Moreover, the quasiequation (
26
) is provable from (
27
) using equational logic and (
13
)–(
16
). Therefore, the resulting set of axioms strengthens
\mathsf{Ax}
(
\Cref
rem:proof-props). Repeating this process yields a strengthening of
\mathsf{Ax}
where all quasiequations except (
13
)–(
16
) satisfy
1
.

2.

Suppose that all quasiequations in
\mathsf{Ax}
except (
13
)–(
16
) satisfy
1
. First, by renaming variables if necessary, we may assume that for every
P\Longrightarrow s=t
in
\mathsf{Ax}
, one has
s,t\in\Delta^{*}\{x,y\}
. Replace every quasiequation

P\Longrightarrow s=t

(28)

in
\mathsf{Ax}
not satisfying
2
, that is, with variables distinct from
x,y
in the premise, by the quasiequation

B\Longrightarrow s=t

(29)

where
B
is a finite basis of
P
w.r.t.
x
and
y
(
\Cref
prop:basis). Then (
29
) is valid by
\Cref
prop:basis-valid. Moreover, since
\vdash P\Longrightarrow u=v
for all
u=v
in
B
by (
21
), the quasiequation (
28
) is provable from (
29
). Thus we obtain a strengthening of
\mathsf{Ax}
where all quasiequations except (
13
)–(
16
) satisfy
1
and
2
.

3.

Suppose that all quasiequations in
\mathsf{Ax}
except (
13
)–(
16
) satisfy
1
and
2
, and that
\mathsf{Ax}
contains a quasiequation

P,\,{\medcirc}\,^{l}(y)={\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)\Longrightarrow s=t.

(30)

Then replace this quasiequation with

P,\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(y)\Longrightarrow s=t.

(31)

This quasiequation is valid because
{\medcirc}\,^{l}(y)={\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)
and
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(y)
are semantically equivalent. Moreover, (
30
) is provable from (
31
) via symmetry (
10
), so the resulting set of axioms strengthens
\mathsf{Ax}
. By repeating this process, we end up with a strengthening of
\mathsf{Ax}
where all quasiequations except (
13
)–(
16
) satisfy
1
–
3
.

4.

Suppose that all axioms in
\mathsf{Ax}
except (
13
)–(
16
) satisfy
1
–
3
, and that
\mathsf{Ax}
contains a quasiequation

P,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)=u,{\medcirc}\,^{l}(x)=v\Longrightarrow s=t

(32)

where
u
and
v
are
{\medcirc}
-terms over
\{y\}
and
k\neq l
, say
k<l
. Replace (
32
) with

P,{\medcirc}\,^{l}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{l-k}u,\,{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k+i}(x)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{i}(u)\,(i=0,\ldots,l-k),\,{\medcirc}\,^{l}(x)=v\Longrightarrow s=t.

(33)

This quasiequation is valid because
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)=u
is semantically equivalent to the conjunction of the identities
{\medcirc}\,^{l}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{l-k}u
and
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k+i}(x)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{i}(u)
for
i=0,\ldots,l-k
. Moreover (
32
) is provable from (
33
) using the congruence rule (
12
) of equational logic. Thus the resulting set of axioms strengthens
\mathsf{Ax}
. By repeating this process, we obtain a strengthening of
\mathsf{Ax}
where all quasiequations except (
13
)–(
16
) satisfy
1
–
4
.

5.

Finally, suppose that all axioms in
\mathsf{Ax}
except (
13
)–(
16
) satisfy
1
–
4
, and that
\mathsf{Ax}
contains a quasiequation

P,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(y),\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{m}(y)\Longrightarrow s=t

(34)

where
l\neq m
and
{\medcirc}\,^{l}(y)={\medcirc}\,^{m}(y)
does not appear in
P
. Replace (
34
) with

P,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(y),\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{m}(y),\,{\medcirc}\,^{l}(y)={\medcirc}\,^{m}(y)\Longrightarrow s=t.

(35)

This quasiequation is valid because the conjunction of
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(y)
and
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{m}(y)
is semantically equivalent to the conjunction of
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(y)
,
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{m}(y)
, and
{\medcirc}\,^{l}(y)={\medcirc}\,^{m}(y)
. Moreover, (
34
) is provable from (
35
) using the symmetry (
10
) and transitivity rules (
11
) of equational logic. Thus the resulting set of axioms strengthens
\mathsf{Ax}
. Repeating this process yields a strengthening of
\mathsf{Ax}
where all quasiequations except (
13
)–(
16
) satisfy
1
–
5
, and thus are normalized. ∎

G.3
Finite Axiomatizability

We are now ready to prove the non-existence of finite axiomatizations for the quasiequational theory of discrete sequence algebras. A discrete axiom system
\mathsf{Ax}
(i.e. a set of finitary
\Delta
-quasiequations) is
sound
for discrete sequence algebras if every quasiequation from
\mathsf{Ax}
is valid for discrete sequence algebras, or equivalently, if every quasiequation provable from
\mathsf{Ax}
is valid for discrete sequence algebras. The axiom system
\mathsf{Ax}
is
complete
for discrete sequence algebras if every quasiequation that is valid for discrete sequence algebras has a finite derivation from
\mathsf{Ax}
.

See
23

Proof.

Suppose towards a contradiction that there exists a finite discrete axiom system
\mathsf{Ax}
that is sound and complete for discrete sequence algebras. By
\Cref
prop:normalization, we may assume w.l.o.g. that all quasiequations in
\mathsf{Ax}
except (
13
)–(
16
) are normalized w.r.t. the variables
x
and
y
. The key to the proof is to exploit that, since
\mathsf{Ax}
is finite and all quasiequations in
\mathsf{Ax}
are finitary, for
{\medcirc}
-identities
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(z)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(z)
,
z\in\{x,y\}
, arising in the premise of any quasiequation in
\mathsf{Ax}
, the difference
|k-l|
of the degrees of the
{\medcirc}
-terms is necessarily bounded from above. In other words, we can choose a natural number
N
such that

(P\Longrightarrow s=t)\in\mathsf{Ax}\hskip 9.24994pt\textnormal{and}\hskip 9.24994pt({\medcirc}\,^{k}(z)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(z))\in P\hskip 18.49988pt\text{implies}\hskip 18.49988pt|k-l|<N.

(36)

Fix a variable
a\in\mathsf{Vars}
and consider the following quasiequations:

\displaystyle{\medcirc}\,^{N}(a)=a,\,{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(a)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k+1}(a)\,(0\leq k<N)

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{i}(a)={\medcirc}\,^{j}(a)\hskip 18.49988pt(N\nmid i-j)

(37)

\displaystyle{\medcirc}\,^{N}(a)=a,\,{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(a)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k+1}(a)\,(0\leq k<N)

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow s=t

(38)

where
i,j\geq 0
, and
s,t
ranges over all pairs of terms in
\Delta^{*}\{a\}
such that one of the two terms is a
{\medcirc}
-term and the other contains
{\circleddash}
. Note that all the above quasiequations have the same premise
\overline{P}
. Moreover, they are all valid for discrete sequence algebras since the premise
\overline{P}
semantically implies that
a
represents a flat sequence.
Therefore, by completeness of
\mathsf{Ax}
, each of the quasiequations (
37
) and (
38
) is
\mathsf{Ax}
-provable. Among them, choose a quasiequation

\overline{P}\Longrightarrow s_{\min}=t_{\min}

(39)

with a proof from
\mathsf{Ax}
of minimum height
h_{\min}
. Fix a proof tree of (
39
) of height
h_{\min}
, and let

P\Longrightarrow s=t

(40)

be the axiom from
\mathsf{Ax}\cup\mathsf{Ax}_{=}
used in the last proof step. We now consider several cases, corresponding to the type of axiom used, and demonstrate that in each case a contradiction arises.

Case 1:
(
40
) is the reflexivity axiom (
9
) from
\mathsf{Ax}_{=}
.

Then
s_{\min}
and
t_{\min}
are identical terms, but this not the case for the conclusion of any of the quasiequations (
37
) and (
38
), a contradiction.

Case 2:
(
40
) is the symmetry axiom (
10
) from
\mathsf{Ax}_{=}
.

Then there exists a proof of
\overline{P}\Longrightarrow t_{\min}=s_{\min}
from
\mathsf{Ax}
of height
h_{\min}-1
. This is a quasiequation of the form (
37
) or (
38
), since (
39
) is, contradicting the minimal choice of (
39
).

Case 3:
(
40
) is the transitivity axiom (
10
) from
\mathsf{Ax}_{=}
. Then there exists a term
u
such that

\overline{P}\Longrightarrow s_{\min}=u\hskip 18.49988pt\textnormal{and}\hskip 18.49988pt\overline{P}\Longrightarrow u=t_{\min}

(41)

have a proof from
\mathsf{Ax}
of height less than
h_{\min}
. By definition of (
37
) and (
38
), one of the terms
s_{\min}
or
t_{\min}
is a
{\medcirc}
-term, say
s_{\min}={\medcirc}\,^{k}(a)
(the other case is symmetric). Note that
u
must be a term in the variable
a
: otherwise, since the premise
\overline{P}
contains only terms in
a
, the
\mathsf{Ax}
-provable quasiequation
\overline{P}\Longrightarrow s_{\min}=u
is clearly not valid, contradicting soundness of
\mathsf{Ax}
. We consider two subcases:

Subcase 3.1:

u
contains
{\circleddash}
.

Then
\overline{P}\Longrightarrow s_{\min}=u
is one of the quasiequations (
38
), and it has a proof of height less than
h_{\min}
, which is impossible.

Subcase 3.2:

u={\medcirc}\,^{l}(a)
for some
l\geq 0
.

Then the
\mathsf{Ax}
-provable quasiequations (
41
) take the form

\overline{P}\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{k}(a)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(a)\hskip 18.49988pt\textnormal{and}\hskip 18.49988pt\overline{P}\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{l}(a)=t_{\min}.

Then
t_{\min}
cannot contain
{\circleddash}
, since otherwise the second quasiequation above is of type (
38
) and has a proof tree of height less than
h_{\min}
, which is impossible. Thus
t_{\min}={\medcirc}\,^{m}(a)
for some
m\geq 0
. Since the above two quasiequations have proof trees of height less than
h_{\min}
, they cannot be of the form (
37
), so
N
divides both
k-l
and
l-m
. Thus
N
divides
k-m
. But this is impossible: since (
39
) is the quasiequation

\overline{P}\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{k}(a)={\medcirc}\,^{m}(a)

and thus of the form (
37
), we have that
N
does not divide
k-m
.

Case 4:
(
40
) is the congruence axiom (
12
) from
\mathsf{Ax}_{=}
.

Then there exists a quasiequation

\overline{P}\Longrightarrow u=v

(42)

with a proof of height
h_{\min}-1
such that
\mathsf{f}(u)=s_{\min}
and
\mathsf{f}(v)=t_{\min}
for some
\mathsf{f}\in\{{\circleddash}\,,{\medcirc}\,\}
. Since
s_{\min}
or
t_{\min}
is a
{\medcirc}
-term (say
s_{\min}
), we have
\mathsf{f}={\medcirc}\,
, and moreover
u
is a
{\medcirc}
-term. If the term
v
contains
{\circleddash}
, then (
42
) is a quasiequation of type (
38
), which is impossible because it has a proof of height less than
h_{\min}
. If
v
is a
{\medcirc}
-term, then (
42
) takes the form

\overline{P}\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{i}(a)={\medcirc}\,^{j}(a)

for some
i,j\geq 0
, and
s_{\min}={\medcirc}\,^{i+1}(a)
and
t_{\min}={\medcirc}\,^{j+1}(a)
. Since
i-j=(i+1)-(j+1)
, the quasiequation (
42
) is of type (
37
). This is impossible since it has a proof of height less than
h_{\min}
.

Case 5:
(
40
) is the one of the quasiequations (
13
)–(
16
).

We first note that no substitution instance of the conclusion of (
13
)–(
15
) is a
{\medcirc}
-identity or an equation where one term is a
{\medcirc}
-term and the other contains
{\circleddash}
. Therefore, neither of these quasiequations can be used in the last step of a proof of (
39
). It thus only remains to consider the case where (
40
) is (
16
), i.e.

x={\circleddash}\,(y)\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,(x)=x.

Then there exists a substitution
\sigma
such that

s_{\min}={\medcirc}\,(\sigma(x))\hskip 18.49988pt\textnormal{and}\hskip 18.49988ptt_{\min}=\sigma(x).

Then
\sigma(x)
is necessarily a
{\medcirc}
-term in
\Delta^{*}\{a\}
(otherwise (
39
) cannot be of the form (
37
) or (
38
)). Therefore, we have a proof of

\overline{P}\Longrightarrow\sigma(x)={\circleddash}\,(\sigma(y))

(43)

from
\mathsf{Ax}
of height
h_{\min}-1
, where
\sigma(x)
is a
{\medcirc}
-term in
\Delta^{*}\{a\}
and
{\circleddash}\,(\sigma(y))
contains
{\circleddash}
. Note that
\sigma(y)\in\Delta^{*}\{a\}
, for otherwise the quasiequation (
43
) is not valid, contradicting soundness of
\mathsf{Ax}
. Therefore, (
43
) is a quasiequation of type (
38
), contradicting the minimal choice of (
39
).

Case 6:
(
40
) is a quasiequation from
\mathsf{Ax}
and none of (
13
)–(
16
).

Then (
40
) is normalized, i.e. satisfies
1
–
5
of
\Cref
def:normalized. Moreover, there exists a substitution
\sigma
such that

s_{\min}=s[\sigma]\hskip 18.49988pt\textnormal{and}\hskip 18.49988ptt_{\min}=t[\sigma],

(44)

and

\mathsf{Ax}\vdash\overline{P}\Longrightarrow u[\sigma]=v[\sigma]\hskip 18.49988pt\text{for all $(u=v)\in P$}

(45)

with respective proofs from
\mathsf{Ax}
of height less than
h_{\min}
. We start with a simple observation; in the following, we write
{\medcirc}\,^{*}
for
{\medcirc}\,^{p}
if the specific exponent
p
does not matter .

(
\star
)
Claim.
If
z\in\{x,y\}
and
\sigma(z)={\medcirc}\,^{*}(a)
, then
P
contains no identity
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(z)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(z)
where
k\neq l
.

Proof.
Suppose that
P
contains some identity
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(z)={\medcirc}\,^{l}(z)
where
k,l\geq 0
, and let
\sigma(z)={\medcirc}\,^{j}(a)
. Then, by (
45
) we have that
\mathsf{Ax}\vdash\overline{P}\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{k+j}(a)={\medcirc}\,^{l+j}(a)
with a proof of height less than
h_{\min}
. This means that this quasiequation is not of type (
37
), so
N
divides
(k+j)-(l+j)=k-l
. On the other hand, we have
|k-l|<N
by the choice (
36
) of the upper bound
N
, and so necessarily
k-l=0
, i.e.
k=l
. This proves (
\star
).

Since (
39
) is a quasiequation of type (
37
) or (
38
), at least one of the terms
s_{\min}
or
t_{\min}
is a
{\medcirc}
-term; by symmetry, we assume that
s_{\min}
is a
{\medcirc}
-term. Since
s_{\min}=s[\sigma]
, also
s
is a
{\medcirc}
-term:

s={\medcirc}\,^{i}(z)\hskip 18.49988pt\text{for some $i\geq 0$ and $z\in\{x,y\}$}.

(46)

Moreover, we have

\sigma(z)={\medcirc}\,^{j}(a)\hskip 18.49988pt\text{for some $j\geq 0$}\,

so that

s_{\min}={\medcirc}\,^{i+j}(a).

Choose an upper bound to the degrees of
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,
-identities in
P
, that is, a natural number
K
such that, for all
v,w\in\{x,y\}
,

({\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{k}(v)={\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,^{l}(w))\in P\hskip 18.49988pt\text{implies}\hskip 18.49988ptk,l\leq K.

(47)

We now consider several subcases, corresponding to whether the variable
z\in\{x,y\}
of (
46
) is
x
or
y
, and to which kinds of identities occur in
P
.

Subcase 6.1:

z=x
, and
P
contains some identity
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{m}(y)
where
k,m\geq 0
.

Let us make the following observations:

[O1.]

1.

P
contains no non-trivial identity
{\medcirc}\,^{*}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{*}(x)
. This follows from (
\star
) since
\sigma(x)={\medcirc}\,^{*}(a)
.

2.

We have
\sigma(y)={\medcirc}\,^{*}(a)
. Indeed,
\sigma(y)
cannot contain a variable
b\neq a
, since otherwise the quasiequation
\overline{P}\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{k}(\sigma(x))={\medcirc}\,^{m}(\sigma(y))
, which is
\mathsf{Ax}
-provable by (
45
), is clearly not valid, contradicting the soundness of
\mathsf{Ax}
. Moreover, it is not possible that
\sigma(y)=u
for a term
u
using
{\circleddash}
, since then we have a proof of
\overline{P}\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{k}(\sigma(x))={\medcirc}\,^{m}(\sigma(y))
of height less than
h_{\min}
and this quasiequation is of type (
38
), a contradiction.

3.

P
contains no non-trivial identity
{\medcirc}\,^{*}(y)={\medcirc}\,^{*}(y)
. This follows from O
2
and (
\star
).

4.

The identity
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{m}(y)
is the unique identity of type
{\medcirc}\,^{*}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{*}(y)
in
P
. Indeed, suppose that there is another such identity
{\medcirc}\,^{l}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{n}(y)
where
(k,m)\neq(l,n)
. By the normalization condition
4
we know that
k=l
. Therefore
m\neq n
, and by
5
the identity
{\medcirc}\,^{m}(y)={\medcirc}\,^{n}(y)
lies in
P
, contradicting O
3
.

In summary, we have shown that
P
contains only one non-trivial
{\medcirc}
-identity, namely
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{m}(y)
. Fix a countably infinite set

A=\{\bot,a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},\ldots\}

(48)

and define the interpretation
\mathfrak{I}\colon\{x,y\}\to A^{\omega}
by

\mathfrak{I}(x)=\bot^{K+k}a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}\cdots\hskip 18.49988pt\textnormal{and}\hskip 18.49988pt\mathfrak{I}(y)=\bot^{K+m}a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}\cdots

(49)

where
K
is the upper bound on the degrees of
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,
-identities in
P
given by (
47
). Then all identities in
P
are satisfied under
\mathfrak{I}
: for the identity
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{m}(y)
this is clear, and all
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,
-identities in
P
are satisfied because they refer to the first
K
elements of
\mathfrak{I}(x)
and
\mathfrak{I}(y)
, which have value
\bot
.

We now look at the conclusion of the axiom (
40
), recalling that
s={\medcirc}\,^{i}(x)
:

Subsubcase 6.1.1:
(
40
) is of the form
P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{i}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{p}(x)
where
p\geq 0
.

Then (
39
) is the quasiequation
\overline{P}\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{i}(\sigma(x))={\medcirc}\,^{p}(\sigma(x))
, i.e.
\overline{P}\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{i+j}(a)={\medcirc}\,^{p+j}(a)
since
\sigma(x)={\medcirc}\,^{j}(a)
. Thus it is a quasiequation of type (
37
), and so
N
does not divide
(i+j)-(p+j)=i-p
. On the other hand, since all identities in
P
are satisfied under
\mathfrak{I}
and the axiom (
40
) is valid, it follows that
{\medcirc}\,^{i}(\mathfrak{I}(x))={\medcirc}\,^{p}(\mathfrak{I}(x))
. But by definition of
\mathfrak{I}(x)
, this is only possible if
i=p
. In particular
N
divides
i-p
, a contradiction.

Subsubcase 6.1.2:
(
40
) is of the form
P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{i}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{p}(y)
where
p\geq 0
.

Since all identities in
P
are satisfied under
\mathfrak{I}
and the axiom (
40
) is valid, it follows that
{\medcirc}\,^{i}(\mathfrak{I}(x))={\medcirc}\,^{p}(\mathfrak{I}(y))
. By definition of
\mathfrak{I}(x)
and
\mathfrak{I}(y)
, this is only possible if

k-m=i-p.

Now consider the quasiequation (
39
), i.e.
\overline{P}\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{i}(\sigma(x))={\medcirc}\,^{p}(\sigma(y))
. We have
\sigma(x)={\medcirc}\,^{j}(a)
and
\sigma(y)={\medcirc}\,^{q}(a)
for some
q\geq 0
, the latter by O
2
. Thus (
39
) is of the form (
37
), which means that
N
does not divide
(i+j)-(p+q)
. Now by (
45
) the quasiequation
\overline{P}\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{k}(\sigma(x))={\medcirc}\,^{m}(\sigma(y))
has a proof from
\mathsf{Ax}
of height less than
h_{\min}
. This quasiequation is equal to

\overline{P}\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{k+j}(a)={\medcirc}\,^{m+q}(a),

(50)

and we have

(k+j)-(m+q)=(k-m)+(j-q)=(i-p)+(j-q)=(i+j)-(p+q).

Thus
N
also does not divide
(k+j)-(m+q)
, which means that (
50
) is a quasiequation of type (
37
) with a proof of height less than
h_{\min}
, a contradiction.

Subsubcase 6.1.3:
(
40
) is of the form
P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{i}(x)=t
where
t
contains
{\circleddash}
.

Since all identities in
P
are satisfied under
\mathfrak{I}
and (
40
) is valid, it follows that
{\medcirc}\,^{i}(\mathfrak{I}(x))={{}^{\mathfrak{I}}\llbracket t\rrbracket}
. But this is impossible: the sequence
{{}^{\mathfrak{I}}\llbracket t\rrbracket}
is flat (since
t
contains
{\circleddash}
) but the sequence
{\medcirc}\,^{i}(\mathfrak{I}(x))
is not.

Subcase 6.2:

z=y
, and
P
contains some identity
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{m}(y)
where
k,m\geq 0
.

Using arguments symmetric to Subcase 6.1, one shows that

[O1.]

1.

P
contains no non-trivial identity
{\medcirc}\,^{*}(y)={\medcirc}\,^{*}(y)
.

2.

\sigma(x)={\medcirc}\,^{*}(a)
.

3.

P
contains no non-trivial identity
{\medcirc}\,^{*}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{*}(x)
.

4.

The identity
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{m}(y)
is the unique identity of type
{\medcirc}\,^{*}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{*}(y)
in
P
.

Therefore
P
contains only one non-trivial
{\medcirc}
-identity. The rest of the argument is exactly like in Subcase 6.1.

Subcase 6.3:

z=x
, and
P
contains no identity
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{m}(y)
where
k,m\geq 0
.

We first observe that

[O1.]

1.

P
contains no non-trivial identity
{\medcirc}\,^{*}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{*}(x)
. This follows from (
\star
) since
\sigma(x)={\medcirc}\,^{j}(a)
.

Take the set
A
given by (
48
) and define the interpretation
{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}\colon\{x,y\}\to A^{\omega}
by

{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}(x)=\bot^{K}a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}\cdots\hskip 18.49988pt\textnormal{and}\hskip 18.49988pt{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}(y)=\bot^{\omega}

(51)

Then all identities in
P
are satisfied under
{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}
: all
{\circleddash}\,{\medcirc}\,
-identities are satisfied because they refer only to the first
K
elements of
{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}(x)
and
{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}(y)
by (
47
), and all non-trivial
{\medcirc}
-identities are of the form
{\medcirc}\,^{*}(y)={\medcirc}\,^{*}(y)
, and they are satisfied because
{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}(y)
is a flat sequence.

We now consider the axiom (
40
) and distinguish three cases:

Subsubcase 6.3.1:
(
40
) is of the form
P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{i}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{p}(x)
where
p\geq 0
.

Then (
39
) is the quasiequation
\overline{P}\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{i}(\sigma(x))={\medcirc}\,^{p}(\sigma(x))
, i.e.
\overline{P}\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{i+j}(a)={\medcirc}\,^{p+j}(a)
since
\sigma(x)={\medcirc}\,^{j}(a)
. Thus it is a quasiequation of type (
37
), and so
N
does not divide
(i+j)-(p+j)=i-p
. On the other hand, since (
40
) is valid and all identities in
P
are satisfied under the interpretation
{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}
, it follows that
{\medcirc}\,^{i}({\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}(x))={\medcirc}\,^{p}({\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}(x))
. But by definition of
{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}(x)
, this is only possible if
i=p
. In particular
N
divides
i-p
, a contradiction.

Subsubcase 6.3.2:
(
40
) is of the form
P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{i}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{p}(y)
where
p\geq 0
.

Since (
40
) is valid and all identities in
P
are satisfied under the interpretation
{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}
, it follows that
{\medcirc}\,^{i}({\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}(x))={\medcirc}\,^{p}({\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}(y))
. But this is impossible because
{\medcirc}\,^{p}({\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}(y))
is flat and
{\medcirc}\,^{i}({\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}(x))
is not.

Subsubcase 6.3.3:
(
40
) is of the form
P\Longrightarrow{\medcirc}\,^{i}(x)=t
where
t
contains
{\circleddash}
.

Since (
40
) is valid and all identities in
P
are satisfied under the interpretation
{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}
, it follows that
{\medcirc}\,^{i}({\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}(x))={{}^{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}\llbracket t\rrbracket}
. But this is impossible: the sequence
{{}^{\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}\llbracket t\rrbracket}
is flat (since
t
contains
{\circleddash}
) but
{\medcirc}\,^{i}({\overline{\mathfrak{I}}}(x))
is not.

Subcase 6.4:

z=y
, and
P
contains no identity
{\medcirc}\,^{k}(x)={\medcirc}\,^{m}(y)
where
k,m\geq 0
.

Analogous to Subcase 6.3.

This concludes the proof of
\Cref
thm:incompleteness.
∎

Appendix H
Appendix: Infinitary Complete Axiomatization (
\Cref
sec:infinitary-ax)

Let us begin our approach towards the completeness proof for
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{\omega}
.

H.1
Completeness of Infinitary Quasiequational Logic

As stated, it appears that the completeness of well-founded derivations for infinitary Horn logic is folklore.
We include a proof here for convenience’s sake, although we make no claim for originality.

See
25

Proof.

We are only going to cover the case where
I
is always a countable set, since this is the case relevant to
{\mathsf{AIC}}_{\omega}
.
However, our proof extends to the general case by replacing
\omega_{1}
with another regular cardinal that is an upper bound on the cardinalities of the index sets
I
that appear in quasiequations in
\mathsf{Ax}
.

(
\Longrightarrow
) Let us begin with
soundness
.
Suppose that
\textup{{q}}=(\bigwedge_{i\in I}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{i}}}}s^{{\kern-5.35158pt{i}\kern 3.68228pt}}_{{\kern-3.33229pt\kern 3.68228pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{i}}}}s^{{\kern-5.35158pt{i}\kern 3.68228pt}}_{{\kern-3.33229pt\kern 3.68228pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{i}}}}s^{{\kern-3.3024pt{i}\kern 2.06718pt}}_{{\kern-1.7172pt\kern 2.06718pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{i}}}}s^{{\kern-2.71178pt{i}\kern 1.47656pt}}_{{\kern-1.12657pt\kern 1.47656pt}}}=\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{i}}}}t^{{\kern-4.67345pt{i}\kern 3.00415pt}}_{{\kern-2.65416pt\kern 3.00415pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{i}}}}t^{{\kern-4.67345pt{i}\kern 3.00415pt}}_{{\kern-2.65416pt\kern 3.00415pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{i}}}}t^{{\kern-2.82771pt{i}\kern 1.5925pt}}_{{\kern-1.24251pt\kern 1.5925pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{i}}}}t^{{\kern-2.37271pt{i}\kern 1.1375pt}}_{{\kern-0.7875pt\kern 1.1375pt}}})\Longrightarrow s=t

is a quasiequation such that
\mathsf{Ax}\vdash_{\mathsf{wf}}\textup{{q}}
, and that
{\mathcal{A}}
is an AIC-structure with
{\mathcal{A}}\models\mathsf{Ax}
. We need to prove
{\mathcal{A}}\models\textup{{q}}
. Let
D
be a well-founded derivation of
q
. We show more generally that for every node of
D
with label
s^{\prime}=t^{\prime}
, we have
{\mathcal{A}}\models\textup{{q}}^{\prime}
where
\textup{{q}}^{\prime}=(\bigwedge_{i\in I}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{i}}}}s^{{\kern-5.35158pt{i}\kern 3.68228pt}}_{{\kern-3.33229pt\kern 3.68228pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{i}}}}s^{{\kern-5.35158pt{i}\kern 3.68228pt}}_{{\kern-3.33229pt\kern 3.68228pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{i}}}}s^{{\kern-3.3024pt{i}\kern 2.06718pt}}_{{\kern-1.7172pt\kern 2.06718pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{i}}}}s^{{\kern-2.71178pt{i}\kern 1.47656pt}}_{{\kern-1.12657pt\kern 1.47656pt}}}=\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{i}}}}t^{{\kern-4.67345pt{i}\kern 3.00415pt}}_{{\kern-2.65416pt\kern 3.00415pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{i}}}}t^{{\kern-4.67345pt{i}\kern 3.00415pt}}_{{\kern-2.65416pt\kern 3.00415pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{i}}}}t^{{\kern-2.82771pt{i}\kern 1.5925pt}}_{{\kern-1.24251pt\kern 1.5925pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{i}}}}t^{{\kern-2.37271pt{i}\kern 1.1375pt}}_{{\kern-0.7875pt\kern 1.1375pt}}})\Longrightarrow s^{\prime}=t^{\prime}
. The proof is by well-founded induction.
